ASK FOR MC DOUCALL'S PATENT SELF RAF INC FLOUR IT IS THE BEST. THE EXAMINER, _SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 20, 1913. 1 FARM AND GARDEN. SUFFRAGE NOTES.

-Old aLtar CHICALA A DRY FEED FOR CHICKENS

DAIRY COWS AND WATERY FOODS..

There is no subject connected with live stock management of greater importance than that which relates to tne best rations for dairy cows, especially, of course, during the winter months, and there is none on which greater uncertainty exists in the minds of the practical farmer. In these days the question of richness in butter fat has come forcibly to the front, and many a farmer will now hesitate to use a food which he knows will increase the flow if he is told that the percentage -of fat Will thereby be reduced. Experiments carried out by competent per- sons to clear up such points, have a very special value, and the results cannot be too widely published. Many of tbese have ,lately been undertaken, and there is a consensus of opinion that the quality of the milk depends far more largely on the character of the cows than on the foods which they consume. A few leading points .are :—(1) A moderate allowance of brewers' grains produces a material increase in the yield, and only in the early period of lactation slightly tends to reduce the percentage of fat. They have no effect on the solids-not- fat. The best time to use them is when the period of lactation is well advanced. Though they do not, in the long run, appreciably affect the quality, it may be well to avoid them when the milk is dangerously near the 3 per cent. minimum. (2) It is now well established that .rations containing a large quantity of water do not decrease the percentage of fat, but, on the other hand, the excessive drinking of water does not increase the flow. (3) \Then cows are on good pasture,there is no, advantage in giv- ing them concentrated food, so far as the quan- tity and fiChness of their milk is concerned.

GRANTS FOR HEAVY HORSES. The Board of Agriculture are to make grants to heavy horse societies on these conditions: (1) No grants shall be given to societies which hire stallions to travel at a fee exceeding £3 3s. (2) In no case shall the grant to a society exceed £BO for each approved stallion provided by it, of which.not -more' than £4O may be a- direct grant, the remainder being Utilised, if necessary, for "assisted nominations." (3) Ex- cept in the case of "assisted nominations" no reduction in the amount of the service fee usually charged shall be made by the societies receiving grants. (4) The stallions ,hired by societies receiving grants shall be approved by competent experts, and registered under the Board's scheme for the registration of stallions; and the mares for which assisted nominations are given shall be approved by the society, as suitable for the ,purpose. (5) The rules of the societies receiving grants shall be approved by the Board.

FOWL TUBERCULOSIS.

Midical Men Now Holding Post-mortem

Examinations of the Birds,

===== 0002601_19130920_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
DOES GEESE-REARING PAY?

Tuberculosis has been found to exist in fowls, and a London medical officer of health makes the following startling announcement: Atten- tion is being directed to the prevalence of the disease in poultry by making a post-mortem examination of emaciated fowls, condemned as unfit for food, in the city markets. Those ex- ambfations are proceeding, and at present it

Is sufficient to state that 16 per cent. of poor quality fowls are affected with tufrerculosis, and. in a degree yhich requires to be seen in order to realise its importance.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
EGGS IN SUMMER.

Some people say that it does not pay to pro- duce eggs in summer, when the price falls so ridiculously low. That is not correct, says "The small Holder." IT is true that it would not pay to buy hens for laying just now, but if You have the hens you may ag well produce the eggs, even though you can only get a half-. Penny each for them. As a matter of fact, nearly every hen will lay just now, and do it Upon much less food than she would require in winter. If you feed in the same manner as you did three or four months ago you will soon get the birds too fat; they will cost you more, and You will get a smaller retqrn. Though eggs realise only about a third of the winter value, they should be produced at very'nearly half the cost,

CLEANLINESS AMONG YOUNG FOWLS. Cleanliness in all things appertaining to Young fowls is of the greatest importance, and unless this is attended to, it is small wonder if they suffer a serious drawback. By cleanliness is not only meant the regular cleaning of the house, feeding and drinking utensils, but in other directions, which are more widespread and drastic in their effect. Especially do para- sites give trouble. It will thus be seen that uniess the most rigid attention be given to cleanliness they will infest the birds to an enormous extent. The presence of these min- ute creatures not only causes a great amount Of suffering by irritation, but they weaken the constitution by the drawing of blood from the body. The birds should be periodically exam- ined, and if troubled in this direction immedi- ate steps should be taken to eradicate the evil.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
GEESE BECOMING POPULAR.

The farmer should be able to find room for several flocks of goslings. If he does riot want the trouble of raising them in the early stages he can buy them half-grown. There is no speCtal breed required, no occasion to go in for the pure variety, either Emden or Toulouse; a cross between these two breeds will prove quite as-useful for fattening purposes. Goslings half- grown (says a writer In "Farm and Home") can be bought ar about Bs. per couple---large, fully-feathered birds, and as they Will live en- tirely on grass, sleep, out in the Open, with- stand all weather conditions, and still suffer no ill-effects, It will be seen that 100 goslings. do not require as much looking after as a single brood of chickens, and provided that none of them are stolen or worried by dogs, there can- not but be a nice balance on the right side. For years many report S have been circulated that geese were not being bred in large numbers, that those who bred them extensively had great difficulty in disposing of them at a profitable rate. Such statements do harm, they make people fight shy of them; yet it is known by those in the trade that the breeding of geese. Instead of going backward, is looking up, that much higher prices prevail than in the past. In seasons when there is little grass, goslings cannot be kept in big numbers even by farmers, but now when there is plenty of grass prac- tically In all fields there is no reason why several flocks should not be kept.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
GREEN GEESE FOR MICHAELMA.

Green geese at Michaelmas are still popular. They may have to be retailed at 6d. per lb. live weight, but they should weigh at least 281 b. to 321 h. per couple. Very little can be put down to the cost of feeding, nothing whatever for labour, so I wonder where there is any live stock that will leave the same profit in the same time and on such a small outlay. Surely, then, the time has come when all those who have ample facinties should wake up to the fact that they are not taking full advantage of the opportunities offered them. If it is contended there will be some difficulties in selling, we have but to ask the large poulterers in the big towns. They tell us candidly that, even at Michaelmas,, green geese are good to dispose of. They will take them dead or alive, of course always pav- ing better prices for geese which are pluck4d and trussed. Although the plucking of geese is something which, personally, I do not care for, still if the best prices are to be obtained the birds must be plucked and trussed, and if a private connection can be obtained, then even higher pries will result. There should not 'be any difficulty in disposing of the spare geese at Michaelmas, and the others can be run on until Christmas, when high prices will be ob- tained for them.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
PROFIT FROM CHESHIRE POULTRY.

A discussion took place at a meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee at Crewe on Monday on the poultry demonstrations whith have been recently conducted jointly by the County Agricultural Committee and the Board of Agriculture at Ilaslington Hall by the Board of Agriculture's expert (Mr: Poynter). The Cheshire Education Committee had written to the Board,. of Agriculture suggesting that Mr. Paynter's services might be continued in Che- shire, but a reply had been received that the Board do not at present contemplate a continu- ance of the demonstrations in Cheshire, as other county authorities had applied for Mr. Paynter's services. Mr. Allsebrook, a Small Holdings Commissioner, sent a report that the demonstrations carried out by Mr. rtynter had been a great success, and had more than real- ised the anticipations of the Board. Over 500 persons had visited the demonstrations, and he strongly urged that the County Council should make provision for continuing the work on similar lines, so that the matter might be still further brought under the notice of small holders and persons interested in poultry-keep- ing. He further stated that the, Board- would be prepared to assist to the extent of 70 per cent, in the equipping and maintenance of such a centre. It was decided that inquiries be made for a suitable centre at which the demonstra- tions in poultry-keeping may be, taken up tem- porarily until such time as thorough provision for this work can be made at the proposed farm dairy institute. Dr. Hodgson said they hoped to prepare a scheme for the demonstrations to be continued on one of the small hafdings.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
GRANTS FOR BULLS.

The Board are also to Make grants for bulls on the following conditions (1) No grant ex- ceeding £l2 per annum is to be made to any Individual bull owner, or exceeding £l5 per annum to any club or society. (2) Not more than four annual grants of £l2 are to be made to any individual; and not more than five an- nual grants of £l5 to dhy society for each approved bull provided by it. (3) Grants are only to be made to individuals when the live stock officer in the area concerned is satisfied, after full inquiry, that it is not possible to form a bull club for a district in which the provision of a good bull is necessary. (4) No grant is to be made to any individual in respect of a bull owned by him before this scheme comes into operation unless the live stock officer is satisfied that in return for the grant the bull can and Will be made available to an appreciably greater 'number of cows belonging to small farmers than it now serves. (5) Not more than one- third of the sum' available for grants in any one year is to be spent in grants to individuals.

" IN THE GARDEN. •

Plant out spring .calThage in places recently occupied with 'peas, eorly potatoesot onions. Cut the Central flower spikes Of -pentsteinons when exhausted, oild encourage secondary shOots. . 2.

Plants in the herbaceous border need atten- tion, cutting off faded blossoms, and staking arid tying top-heavy growths. Pinch off the tops of runner beans and keep the pods closely picked. When the last lot of peas is gathered, cut off the tops and burn them. Leave the roots in the ground. Cut away old raspberry canes and reduce the young suckers to About half a dozen, tying them loosely in to prevent' the growths from becoming twisted. Bare places on grass should be pricked with a fork, and grass seed should be sown. Fresh lawns may be made now, selecting showery, or dull weather, for the purpose.

.Divide clumps of Christmas roses,(Helleborus Niger) and plant in rather shady position in a rich loam if possible.

Fill up vacant places in the garden with chrysanthemums for reserve beds. There are, always places in the garden at this part of the year which _would be much improved by a 'mum. They 'transplant well if they are cut round with a spade a day or two before lifting, and given a good watering. Take cuttings of verbenas of the choicer sorts. Pull off the short flowerWss side shoots with a heel, and insert in boxes filled with loam, leaf soil and sand. Frame protection will be necessary.

POISONOUS GARDEN PLANTS.

Flowers of the jonquil, white hyacinth and snowdrop all possess a poisonous nature, the . narcissus being also 'particularly deadly, so inuch indeed that to Chew a small scrap of one of the bulbs may result fatally, while the juice of the leaves is an emetic. The berries of the yew have killed_ many persons, and it is pretty well known .noWadays that it is. not safe to eat many peach pips or cherry kernels at once. The lobelias are all dangerous. Lady's slipper poisons in the same manner as does poison ivy. Me bulbs seem to be the most harmful. Lilies of tile valley are also as much so, says the "Family Doctcir." There is enough opium, in red .poppks to do mischief; and the autumn crocus, if the blossoms are chewed, cause vomit- ing and purging. The leaves and flowers of the oleander are deadly, and the .bark of the catalpa tree is very mischievous; the water drop wpft, when not in, flower,' resembles celery, and is virulent. ' lIARDY PEREN:IALS FROM SEED.

Advantage should be taken of the moister conditions now obtaining to sow patches of perennials for furnishing the borders. Although seed may be sown in the open in shady posi- tions, preference should be given to boxes, as they can be moved about at will from shade to sun and vice versa. Thp soil covering can be better gauged, as some of the seeds are tiny. To enable the seeds to germinate and the re- sultant plants come on well a good rooting soil is necessary. Plenty of leaf mould and some sharp sand should be mixed with ordinary gar- den soil. When the soil is in the boxes give a good watering, and sow the seeds the following day. Cover the seed 'evenly, the depth accord- ing to the size of the. seeds. Some will require the barest covering. Shade is necessary until germination takes place, and even then they must not be left all day in bright sunshine. The boxes will be far better left entirely in the shade rather, than in bright sunshino,

Will Greater Freedom Lead to Neglect of Womanly Duties?

SERVICE VERSUr SUBJECTION.

BY A CORRESPONDENT.

The idea of the benefit which the State may derive from the introduction into public life of the maternal point of view is well set forth in a Pamphlet entitled "Service versus Subjection," lately published by the National Union. There is a widespread fallacy that the movement for the greater fre.edom of women must lead to the neglect of those duties and sacrifices which have always been associated with womanhood. But the Woman's Movement does not demand freedom from duty; it demands fuller oppor- tunities for service. To serve, however, as the writer of the pamphlet points out, "is not the same as to be subject.' She goes on to wel- come, as an advance from the old point of view, " the greater emphasis laid in these days on motherhood, as compared with wifehood, as the fruition of a woman's life," and to show that whereas the woman who has made wifehood her Ideal and ahn, if she remains unmarried, can have but a stunted and unsatisfied exist- ence, and feel that there is no place for her in the world, "the woman who has taken mother- hood as her ideal can be of service to her country in a hundred ways,.apd lead a full .and 'happy life, even if she has no children of her own. There are many ways of giving life, besides the giving of physical lire; and many ways of promoting and strengthening a life already existing."

===== 0002601_19130920_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
A DRAG UPON THE MOVEMENT.

Constitutional Suffragists, says a writer in the "Common Cause, are all opposed to acts. of violence and illegality-740 what.may be called conventional. militancy—but have they not all met, and within their 'own ranks, the exasper- ated mind whose tendency is to strike out at existing conditions, rather than find patience to achieve their gradual modification? The move- ment has been a success just in so far as bitter- ness has been eliminated and justice done, as well as demanded. After all, what has asperity to do with beliefs like ours? Our Suffragist faith Is rooted in a conviction it, the endless possibilities open to the human race, and what place has bitterness in a creed like that? Our work is to see to it that we do not in ourselves create the obstacles which retard the evolution- ary process; to remember that a person who has lost faith in human nature ,has already become a drag upon the movement. It is only - through living hearts, where the current of human sympathies seems strong and free and unembittered, that the supreme glory of the Suffrage movement—the • making of a human soul—can he achieved. It has been truly said that the contagion of religion is nearly always a matter of personal friendship, and it is surely equally true that the contagion of the Suffragist Spirit is most often communicated by those who have learned how to render themselves, not only convincing, but, In the deepest ,sense of the word, beloved.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMAN SUFFRAGE IN AMERICA.

Washington cannot have failed to be pressed last month with the strength of the Suffrage movement throughout the U.S.A., for representatives of , nearly 4,000,000 women voters iaet° there in Council to discuss the best way of promoting the enfranchisement apt' women in those States, where the Suffrage has not yet been granted. It is probable -that an International Confer- ence of Women Voters will be held at San FranciSco hi 1915. This would do much to bring home to the general public the results whicn may be expected from the women's vote. From every State where the franchise has been won comes a report of solid work accomplished, and of the banding together of women to obtain hirther legislation for the.Acalth 'of the nation, ttind the protection of the -Weak and oppressed. .Laws for the welfare of Children, shorter hours and improved Conditions of women's work, proper provision for the sick, decent housing accommodation; and the rooting out of Com- mercialised vice, are resulting all over the world from the Woman's Movement; and better administration is also being secured. It is frequently pointed out by Opponents of Woman Suffrage that legislation on these lines is passed by Parliaments responsible only to men. This must be admitted; but such laws are passed far more . quickly, in countries where women Vote, and we maintain that even where women are still unenfranchised, the agitation for the Suffrage has called attention to many questions hitherto overlooked. In spite of all restrictions, the influence of women's mothering instinct has made itself felt In public life. But what a vast amount of devo- tion and service has been lost to the nation by the cramping and confining of women's energy!

WHAT DYSPEPTICS SHOULD

EAT.'

A PHYSICIAN'S ADVICE.

"Indigestion and practically all forms of stomach trouble are, nine times out of ten, due to acidity; therefore stomach sufferers should, whenever possible, avoid eating fcibd that is acid in its nature, or which by chemical action in the stomach develops acidity. Unfortunately, such a rule eliminates most foods which are pleasant to the taste as well as those which are rich in 'blood, flesh and nerve building properties. This is the reason why dyspeptics and stomach suf- ferers are usually so thin, emaciated and lacking in that vital energy which can only come from a well-fed body. For the benefit of those suf- ferers who have been obliged to exclude from itheir diet all starchy, sweet or fatty food, and are trying to keep up a miserable existence on gluten products, I would suggest that you should try a meal of any food or foods which you may like in moderate amount, taking imme- diately afterwards half a teaspoonful of bisur- ated naagnesia in a little hot or cold water. This will neutralise any acid which may be present, or which may be formed, and instead of the usual feeling of uneasiness and fulness, you will find that your food agrees with you perfectly. Bisurated magnesia is doubtless the best, food corrective and antatid known. It is not a medicine, and has no direct action. on the stomach; but by neutralising the acidity of the food contents, and thus removing the source of the acid irritation which inflames the delicate stomach lining, it does more than could possibly, be done by any drug or medicine. As a physi- cian I believe in the use of medicine whenever necessary, but I must admit that I cannot see the sense of dosing an inflamed and irritated stomach with drugs instead of getting rid of the acid—the cause of all the trouble. Get a little bisurated magnesia from your chemist, eat what mu want at your next meal. fakn some of the bisurated magnesia as directed above, and see if I'm not right."

===== 0002601_19130920_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
"BY-LAWS" WHICH ARE NOT L.4WS.

_ The man who "knows things" is never tiNd of pointing out that a by-law is not necessarily law, says a writer in " Cassell's Saturday Journal," under the title of "By-laws that are not Laws." And for once in a way he is right. It is not. Hundreds of by-laws are, in fact, worthless, either because, they are repugnant to common law, or because of some fatal oversight in drafting and publishing them. Those shame- less monopolists, railway companieS, have many regulations which. they cannot .compel us to observe. They solemnly warn us, • for in- stance, not to 'smoke at stations on pain of a heavy penalty. Did anybody ever know a man to be lined. for smoking on a platform? An- other unenforceable by-law is that laying down that a passenger who joins a train without a ticket will be required, on reaching his destina- tion, to pay the fare from its starting point:Tor several reasons this regulation is of no value in law; yet there is a widespread belief to the contrary. Many people , who enter a train in such circumstances. at an intermediate station make 6 point of telling the guard that they have not booked—a precaution which, at all events, relieves, them of suspicion of an intent to de- fraud. But this is not really necessary, and still less is it, when travelling ticketless from B to C, to pay the fare from A, twenty or thirty miles beyond B. Nobody can be made to do +anything of the kind: .

===== 0002601_19130920_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
BY A WARRINGTON IRISHMAN.

The Unionists of Ireland, who have grabbed, and now hold nearly all the positions and power worth possessing in that country, once more proclaimed on Friday that whenever the oppor- tunity offered, it was their intention to pursue their course of shamelessness, no matter how much they might offend public susceptibilities, or outrage the rights of their Nationalist fellow countrymen. It was not necessary that they should have made this declaration of their party's policy, as it has never varied from that of one-sidedness and self, but there are times when it is unwise to show too bold a front'und we would venture to assert that the protagon- ists have selected a rather inopportune time for reminding those in political opposition of what might be expected, should Time's wheel give a half-turn in the reactionaries' favour.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
ULSTER ASSOCIATION NOBBLED.

The latest display of these typical "Loyalists" was at the annual meeting of the Ulster Associa- tion in London, which was established that all men of the Northern Province living in Lon- don might have a common ground of meeting, and into which neither religion , nor politics were to be introduced. • This was the original intention of the distinguished founders, who included Lord Russell of Killowen. the Marquis of Dufferin, Mr. Vesey Knox, and many other men of eminence and literary standing. In later days, however, the old spirit of ascend- ancy, so characteristic of the Orange corner of Ireland, asserted itself, after an influx of North- ' Eastern adherents, and took the form of a reso- lution,_moved by a Mr. /41!Grandy, which was ; in substance that the society -should become Unionist—a proposal which was carried on Friday last, after a division. It is only fair to state that such Protestants as Judge Rentoul and Mr. Edwin Woods denounced the proposi- tion, which they saw would create a very dan- gerous precedent, but the cry of " Halt " was in vain to those who have no consideration beyond an apparent_ victory for the time being, and who, on this occasion, were not to be denied their opportunity. They carried their motion and now will have the society to themselves having first of all laid it down as their stan- dard of public morality that whenever the Unionists secure, a majority they are in duty bound to convert whatever society or Council they may be connected with into a part of the political machinery on their side—indeed, in seine cases they have turned theatres and pic- ture palaces into happy hunting-grounds. Should any Nationalists at a future date decide to act upon this rule, they can point to the example set by the Ulster Association of London—with Lord Mayor M`Mordie, M.P., of Belfast, in the chair—and mention the North-East transaction as their guide. When the Association was established it was intended that the member- ship should be confined to men of weight in the world, the founders never contemplating that they would have in their ranks some of the very " undistinguished " persons, who are now on the roll, and in possession of the -vote. Regrettable though this may be to the original members, we are unaware of a protest having been raised on that head, but a very genuine one was addressed to the Chairman by Mr. Vesey Knox, who declared that the proposal before the meeting was "a violation of the fundamental rule of the society, under. which subscriptions of members had been obtained." The money was paid to a non-political and non- sectarian organisation, which has now, by the vote of the Unionist members, been constituted a political body. ,

===== 0002601_19130920_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR NATIONALISTS TO REMEMBER.

This is also something which it is possible not a few on the Nationalist side in Ireland- will keep in mind for the future, and who may write down as a safe axiom that wherever the Unionists of the .North-East. Can assume -con- trol they will give no quarter to a political or religious opponent. The blundering of the Northern bigots, under the Lord Mayor of Bel- fast, has, we observe, been repudiated by the "Globe." which says: "For a piece of gross stupidity and incredible blundering, commend us to the conduct of those who are determined to throw down the gauntlet in the Ulster Association in London, and turn it into a poli- tical body. No one will accuse us of lack of sympathy with the claims of the Northern Pro- vince, but that sympathy does not blind us to the gross ineptitude of those who evidently have gained control of the Ulster Asspciation." Their "gross ineptitude" and • " stuPidity " have come as a timely reminder to the Nation-. alists of Ireland—many of whom were beginning 'to forget all that landlord and Unionist rapacity were responsible for in the past. It is plain, however, from the vote of this Association in London, that when political animosity is driven to the extremes of converting a 'social club into a political factor, that were there any loaves and fishes in question, the majority on such a vote would have been an overwhelming poll to bar out the Nationalists and Catholics from the smallest participation, no matter what their claims. And it is to bolster up this class that Mr. William O'Brien is going into hysterics.

ORANDEISM AND HAVOC.

He wants conciliation and "peace" with the North-East people, who do not know the mean- ing of the words when applied to others, an whose whole history has been one Of ascend- Lky, speaking of the eighteenth century type, says: "We have abundant evi- dence that great numbers of poor men's houses were burnt for slight reasons, and with- out a shadow of legal justification, and there is reason to believe that in the ,midnight raids many persons were shot, probably by the Orangemen, in simple murder. All these things naturally tended to stir up fierce and enduring animosities "—which were dying out until Sir Edward Carson appeared on the scene. It is possible that some of his followers.may believe they shall have a repetition of the scenes described by the Protestant historian, Walpole, who wrote that the Orangemen in 1797 were "encouraged to play havoc with the miserable inhabitants of the proclaimed districts. . . . Houses were plundered and burnt, women out- raged, children brutally ill-treated and mur- dered. Men were seized and sent on board tenders untied. They were flogged, 'picketed,' and half-hung; they were hunted down and sabred; villages and whole districts were devas- tated, and the inhabitants turned out of their homes into the ditch."

They are "loyal" to themselves, they would give no other man a chance, and to-morrow, if they had the power, they would as unct)remoni- ously drive the remainder of the Catholics from Belfast and Portadown. But such actions will reflect upon themselves, and eventually convert others to the side of Ireland's liberty.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
HISTORIC RHYME RECALLED.

The land question was eloquently expounded Upon by Mr. R. Blake; of the Land Reform Council, at the weekly meeting of the Progres- sive Labour League at Bridge Foot on Monday. Councillor J. Shaw presided over a large attendance and he was supported by Mr. J. Johnson and Mr. R. Osborne.

Hypocrisy is the homage that vice pays to virtue, said Mr. BLAKE, and at a certain time in their life the landowners ceased to be hypocrites and started to rob openly. At one time the commons of England were surrounded by land- lords, who, by the infamous Closure Act, each took their slice of ..the land.- Popular indigna- tion -voiced itself in the following lines:

We put in gaol the man or woman, Who steals the goose from-off the common, We let the greater felon loose, Who stole the common from the goose.

That, added Mr. Blake, is true poetry. It is not like that of the late Poet Laureate, whose appointment was a bit of jobbery. He wrote leading articles in the "Standard," the Tory paper, but he could no more write poetry than a pig .can play marbles. (Laughter.) I think those lines ought to be 'rethembered, because they had power in them. I advise you to read Henry George's great book, "Progress and Poverty," for there you will find that Mr. George proves, I don't say he asserts or claims, but proves that beyond all possible shadow of a ,doubt that as long as the. present 'land system continues it does not matter what you do for the benefit of the people, because it will go into rent.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
Landlords Get the Benefit.

After proving that through industry the value of land was greater and that the landowners got the benefits, Mr. Blake said that at the recent London dock strike the "Daily News" raised a subscription for the benefit of the wives and families of the men on strike. One woman received 10s., but out of it she had to pay is. 6d. for two rooms. ("Shame.") That was making a subscription for the slum land- lord, the man who in that time of distress had dared to apply for his rent. A statement made afterwards was that the rents ..had all been punctually paid... He -would have thought it better if the people had refused to pay their rents until the trouble was over.

The old system of landlordism was so far from our modern civilisation and so purely a fossil that he...did not believe it would last another month after -it was Seriously tackled.. People might call me an optimist, he added, to suppose that the system would be over- thrown. I am an optimist and no one is suc- cessful in this world who is not optimistic. It was done in Ireland and landlordism . seemed to be a thousand times more strong there than in England, because they had behind them the Goi•ernment who did all they could for the landlords. But the people of Ireland brought' the landlords to their knees. Some of you will, remember 1881, when the ejectment orders— described by Mr. Gladstone as sentences of death--were served on the Irish people. But they had a great leader behind them, a man who would not wobble. From his cell in gaol Parnell issued that celebrated historic docu- ment, in which he told the people to "keep a grip on your homes and do not pay a penny of your rent until the kind question is equitably settled?! In „three. parts of the country the people obeyed Parnell, and did not pay a penny of rent until Mr. Gladstone settled the question so admirably. The transfer of the land had been accomplished there, and that is the result pf what the Irish people earned for their pertin- acity and courage. II Ireland can do that, why shouldn't we?

===== 0002601_19130920_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
To Make Up Their Minds.

The moment you get the English people to make up their minds that landlordism must be abolished in this country it will go fike a bubble. (A Voice: "is the land good enough for the Irishman to live in?") Well, a working man in Ireland can get a new and modern cottage and half an acre of land for is. 6d. per week. (Applfiuse.) These are the developments we hope' to see in England. The one supreme interest of the State is that the people should live under such conditions that they may main- tain their degree of strength and energy so that the children can carry on our fine and noble traditions. You must get rid of the landlords, but how are we to do it? There are three ways. One is to buy the landlords out, the other is to kick them out—(hear, 'hear)—and the final way is to tax them out. The Tories hate the land valuation which is at present going on. We trust toibuy .the landlords out at the, value which the land is taking for rating purposes. If we want some land and the Ow-ner says ,he wants so much, which we consider exhorbitant, then we shall simply say "Keep it, but we will rate you on those figures now.". The land will be managed in the interests of the public. We want to get possession of the land immediately to put a stop to the evils of private ownership and prevent recontinuation of unearned incre- ment. They did not want to kick the landlords out without compensation. , The line of least resistance is -to ,purchase the land at 6 reason- able- valuation. The process of "kicking out" had been tried. They all remembered the French revolution, The result of that was that French liberty was crushed under the most iron despotism that ever existed.

Do Away With Landlordism.

There are breakers ahead and storms coming In England, and the landlords will only be too glad to get out of possession of the land. urge you to support whichever party is pre- pared to do away with landlordism at the next election. (A Voice: "Do you think the present Liberal party will pass the Land Valuation Bill this Parliament?") "Yes," concluded Mr. Blake, "I think they will, well I hope so." (Ap- plause.)

Mr. •JoHN JOHNSON proposed the following resolution: "That this meeting is of the opinion that the root evil of the present land system consists in its treatment of land as private property, and it strongly urges the Govern- ment, when preparing its promised policy of land reform, to provide for the gradual and equitable acquisition of land by public authori- ties, both local and national, the purchase price being definitely based upon the value as ascer- tained for purposes of taxatiqn." lie ilso pro- posed a. vote of thanks to the speaker. Mr. RICHARD OSBORNE ,seconded, and the 'reso- lutions were Carried unanimously.

WOMEN'S QUESTIONS.

Fashions to be Shown at the Picture Palaces.

SOME REVIVALS.

Some of the leading business houses are making use of the cinema to display a "Gazette of Fashion," which is being shown on the scraens at, picture palaces all over the country. Well-known -actresses are engaged to show off lovely dresses for afternoon and evening wear, and illustrate the latest modes in tennis, sports and golfing suits. A fashion note, descriptive of the costume displayed , is appended to each picture. Possibly this may prove to be an early introduction of the new journalism, which it has been prophesied is coming, \Zen people will not only go to the cinema for amusement, but also to get the news of the day. Newspaper proprietors will not be scared by these predic- tions, but the cinema screen may, of course, be made to serve many useful purposes. In America the portraits of " missing " people have been exhibited, and being recognised by some member of the audience, the discovery of the absent one has in some cases followed, but the same result has often been-obtained by tire p4b- lication of portraits in the newspapers. A REVIVAL IN WOOL WORK.

A return to the old Berlin wool work of our grandmothers is almost a craze just now, and much of the work in old manor and country houses, which frequently contain a wealth of old-time fabrics and furniture, is being copied by expert needlewomen, and employed as cov- erings for chairbacks and seats. The 'work is simple enough in execution, but its effect de- pends on the design and the colours used. A demand for it arose some three years ago, and has been steadily increasing, sufficiently so to warrant the establishment of workshops that deal exclusively with - it. The work was formerly called petikpoint and gros,point, which means a little stitch and a big stitch. An exhibition was held a short time ago in a wen. known studio in the West End, which was crowded with. fashionable women, who were very interested in the sofa cushions, bags, and chairbacks displayed, all worked in Berlin wool. Th.ese hrticles were not, of course, in the old pictorial designs, representing scenes iron' the Old and New Testaments, but in modern and original patterns that came from the brush of an exponent of the English' post-impressionist school.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
I,OST-IMPRESSIONIST FLRNITURE.

This same school having secured a footing_ precarious, perhaps—in the world of dress, in seeking new fields for the exploitation of its decorative art, has turned its attention to fur. niture and furnishing fabrics, and the result will certainly astonish all sober-minded Britons, who still cherish a regard for the com- paratively- sombre hues of mahogany and for more modern but quiet tones, in damasks, reps, and velvets. At the exhibition alluded to above, some rush-seated chairs were shown, treated in the new style, and the result to the uniniti- ated was grotesque in the extreme. The legs, back-slats, rails and upright were each Painted in different vivid colours, and had seats worked in the dosignS,.suppliecl•by the artist for Berlin' wool. A stool was an orgy in colour, the legs were painted bright primrose on one face and sky-blue on the other, a diamond border in black running across the front , rail. It is im- possible to imagine a suburban villa furnished after this styli. WAIST BELTS AND COLLARS, 1

The shapelessne.ss of the fashionable waist demandsia -break in the straight .line that ex- tends frotn shoulder to knee, and.as he waist belt supplies it, niiich ingenuity hig been ex- pended on novelties for the coining' "season. The small waist having completely disappeared, and a mpasuremebt of from twenty-four to twenty.-six inches being the required circumfer- "ence, swathed and pleated belts that give the effect of thickness and even clumsiness to the figure, are in great request. White and coloured leather show geometrical designs. Suede is 'extensively used, some belts of this kind are very deep, and fall as much as six inches below the waist. Buckles are narrow, and many are made of enamel. A large num- ber of the new collars for 'wearing with suits or blouses are hand-embroidered in eyelet holes. They are very attractive, and quite worth while working at home, and as the patterns are not intricate, very little time would be'required to make a set of three or four.

BASQIUED BLVUSES AND A NEW CORSAGE.

Blouses for afternoon, or more dressy, wear have fascinating basques, which are sometimes long enough to be described as tunics; in other instances they may with correctness be termed frills. A novel corsage that falls limp and straight and bears a striking resemblance to a jersey, for it fastens with jewelled buttons on the left shoulder, is made of lace, and lends itself to. home manufacture. Deep lace ilounc- ings, or even the old-fashioned deep lace veils, of rather more than a hundred years ago, which are still in the possession of some fortunate .folks, wopld answer admirably if used for this purpose. A flower at the waist is the only ornament, save the buttons. THE SEASON'S WRAP-COAT.

. The wrap-coat in Velour delaine, or as it is more usually called, Teddy bear plush, is an outstanding feature in the \Vest End shop g of London just now. Cream, yellowish brown, dull blue, Indian red, and mulberry, are only a few of the shades in which these fascinating garments are shown_ ,In the cutting much con- sideration has been given to new lines; giving a drawn-across effect, and striped or ribbed 'surfaces are frequently Cut slightly on the cress in order to produce striking or novel results: The latest models fasten close up to the neck, slightly on the left. The neck is finished with a fur collar, and cuffs appear on the sleeves. For theatre wear, these coats are very service- able. Long coats of black silk and, velvet will be as popular as ever. These show, much variety in shape. The coat of tweed and serge Is also very much in evidence, for it is too use- ful to be dispensed with.

"Blasphemous and Wicked Notion."

"What is duty?" was the theme of the Fe''' Dr. Mellor, preaching, in connection with it series of sermons on 'Morality and the .51101." Life," in Cairo-street Chapel on. Sunday. It is your duty, people say, to love Pult: country, he said, and to. defend it again enemies. Very well; but suppose my gene?, conception of life and of my own place .in it te"l me that war is an evil and barbarous thing, ll° to 13e tolerated in any circumstances, or that nIY country is so detestably mismanaged as not t° he worth defending—what happens to Ell" then? .If I am myself clear as to my own ideal' then obviously it becomes my duty not to defen my country, not to bear arms at all. 31013 people, however, who face such a situationi generally end in the state of not knowing wha ' their duty is—or perhaps of going blindly along with the mass of opinion and doing as the majority do. Most people get along somehow, 1)37, observing the rules of custom, Convention an, respectability, by behaving as average publ,l4o" opinion dictated, by doing what most peon did, in general by going with the stream—nq thus it happens that, whilst there may be nine" talk about duty, there is very little real know- ledge of what duty means, very little concelr tion of what one's personal duty is, very 11tt.1! real facing of the severe meaning and the still more severe problems of any genuine conception of duty. The or(inion seemed to be in modern societY that duty existed on an economic basis, that,a. man's duty was what he was paid to do. things. were, and stripped of all its Actitiouf! glamour, this notion had a peculiarly brat at and sinister appearance—it meant simply tha a man's duty was just what he had to do order to save himself film starvation. On tbal. positively blasphemous and wicked notion of duty . modern industry rested, and it was an' appalling thing to contemplate. "Your dt is what you are paid to do." He said, ,quite' sincerely and solemnly, that that was a . able lie, and an accursed piece of filthy am, slavish cant. 'Duty could not be bought 311.1. • sold. /t was not itself„ nor was the doing o,_f a purelia,sable commodity,- and to suppose ts'l% duty was in any way dependent on an econonn... basis, or measurable in economic terms, utterly to -miss even the barest rudiments 43; morality, and so to make a genuinely moral a1!,9 e dutiful life impossible. Genuine morality wo' ' not, and could not, begin until at least con"; parative economic liberty was assured, and was "SP'hiP to lead a good life without ,having„ to mix their attempt up with the question nu where their next meal was coming from. omic stress was the greatest enemy of moralitY" in the world.

America Has Tackled Tariffs: A Good Omen for Cm' Reformers.

“NO TINKERING.”

We hear 'from America that the new 'Tariff will receive the signature of the President, and conic into operation next month. The etfect of this reform will metui the IVLitIC-• 11011 of the high tariff by something like st; per cent. In this 'We should see a blow- struck at the doctrine of Protection, which has, more or less, engaged the attention of the electors in the last -three General Elections. When a great Protected ,country. like the United Slates can bring about -a revOlution in tariffs such as that We have witnessed in lbss than twelve months, ' l'eformers.of the ,taxing and rating systems of this country 'may, enter on the .next campaign. with new hope. Perhaps it is because America IS so far off many people in this country fail II) realise the enorineus power. of -Trasts and vvs-ted interest, to say 'nothing of land -,mono- -1)(4 generally, thatithe reformers in the United states had to :fight before ..theY succeeded in Making the IlOuse of licpre.sentatives and the • -/;enate at 'Washington of the character and .S'rripathy which are -necessary • when dealing With reform. It was no :easy task ttk -Free Trtide 'democrats' of the United States tinAleV. took after the passing' of the Payne-Aldrich Tariff under President Taft's administration, ' for that Tariff Bill made the trusts stronger and vested interest more arrogant. Nevertheless, Pree Traders, through a mighty effort which Passed all .through the States, formed their 4ocieties and began an educational campaign 41 which speakers, literature, exhibitions and lantern lectures never ceased their work until president Woodrow Wilson was elected with NYmpathMe legislative houses. The odds, the t?ree Traders had-to* fight were enormous. They. had against them. the magnates, who directly benefited from the system of Protection; the ‘'vealth they had amassed, and the machinery c4t elections, indeed, every kind of political Power and Influence to defeat the reformers Y in the hands Of' the tiaiStniongerS. The Evils of Land Monopoly!

Now if such a victory can be gained in the Cnited States agaifist the forces of monopoly 'lnd privilege, why should reformers who wish to overthrow Monopoly and privilege in this 4s'ountry be :downhearted? It is sometimes 441(1 by the enthusiasts for land reform in this cottntry that nothing can lie accomplished on I[l4 economic lines until there sits -at St. Ntephen's a Liberal party which has lost" all vestige of Whiggism. These enthusiasts say 4illat the most difficult opponents of economic vind reform we have to deal with are not in 44 Conservative party, but in the Liberal Party.. - •Whether this be true or not,:let it be 'Ninembered tha.t, the Free - .Trade dertiocrats 4ftew :quite well when ,they, entered on -their (;ztrtipaign that there were many enemies in `l,letr own camp, and they successfully made a ilearance Of opponents in. their ranks. If we Ptre to have a Liberal party determined to f'nrrY out the pledges of our leading Ministers we must have a party behind the friends of telorm in the Cabinet who will support unwav- '4.ringly ,in the house legislation which will 71fectii;/ely .break up' land monopoly. Indeed. ext election should be Sought on this ques- (ll.l; it should hold the field as did the Parlia- ::era Act at the last election; as did the Budget ,1909 at the election of January, 1910. But this is to be done there must be started at a •land 'campaign that will be prosecuted, 111 now on until , the country Is thoroughly tlcated on the evils of land monopoly and the hating and taxing of effort and industry, and the eSt and 'sanest method of reforin. lit is as well to take time by the forelock. The ,trrade, Ileturns for last. month show a falling We learn from other countries that. a ii('riod of trade depression is setting in., Only, Other day articles in German newspapers itt'sed the industrial and financial condition "ecmany;' and sonic of the papers predicted* 41 winter of vast unemployment and severe ~•st,ress• These reports of- industrial decline Ari.7ll European countries should be taken as kvoll storm-clouds gathering on the horizon. Vleti has been clear for two or three years. Nittlei, Germans are already busily engaged on Littuatives, make-shifts and dodges fore. llev- the IlistresseS which are in 'prospect. taje secins to be a 'great body of opinion in 11c of reclaniation of wastes and theiutilisa- irl,", 01 moors and fens for the purpose "of find- -labour for the unemployed: If we are to ;IR f nother period of trade depression, such tirti(:e Passed Throfigh in 1909, let us be wise in aeur, and concentrate on a method of counter- ifs effect by dealing with the land system tt to „Li home,a which is admittedly the worst on of the earth.

1,44, ti Values in Populous Places. those intereted in the taxation of land ' "es will remember the memorial which was tlou""ed by the Land Values Group ui the of Commons to the Prime Minister and Th,ancellor of the Exchequer two years ago. whr Inetnorial set out the nature of the reforms the Land Values Group considered urgent rilst of the proposals v,-ere on the lines

LW FRtCIS NEILSON, M.P.

indicated by Ministers in their speeches at different times during the past 20 years. The memorial urged the Government to hasten the completion of land valuation; to make that valuation public; and 'to levy _a flat tux on all land values without exemption. It also sug- gested that the cost of national burdens such as education, poor law, main roads, police and asylums should be placed upon the National chequer,•and that powers should be.givento. local authorities to rate land values for local services. _

To show how long these questions baye been under censideration it is only necessary to remind your readers of the report of the Royal Commission On the Housing .of the .Weirking Classes WhiCh .sat in 188, 28 years ago! That RoYal Commission reported as :follow s At present land :ivailable for-building .in the neighbourhoOd of our populous centres, though its capital value is very great, is probably pro- ducing a small yearly return until it is let for building. The owners of this land are rated, mit in relation to the real 'value, but to the 'actual annual income. They can thus afford to keep (heir land out of the market, and to part ! with only small quantities, so as to raise the price beyond the actual monopoly price which the land would command by. its advantages of position. Meantime, the general expenditure of the town on improvements is increasing the value of their property. If this land were rated at, say, 4 per cent. of its selling value, the owners would have a more direct incentive to part with it to those who are desirous of build- ing, and a twofold advantage would result to the community ;.first, atithe valuable property would contribute to the rates, and tIMS the bur- den on the occupier would be diminished by the increase in the ratable property. Secondly, the owners of the building land would be forced to offer their land for sale, and thus their coin- petttiOn With one another-Would :bring down the price of building land, and so diminish the tax: in the shape of ground rent or price paid for land, which is now levied on urban enter- prise by the adjacent property-owners—a tax.; be it remembered, which is no recompense for any industry or expenditure on their part, but is the natural result of the industry and activity of the townspeople themselves. His Majesty's Commissioners would recommend that these matter's should be included in legislation, when the law of rating comes to be dealt with by Parliament. , This is a very clear statement ! But no won- der those interested in the question aye become impatient at :the long delay!

Preposterous Inequalities. Inc necessity-.oi Government putting these proposals into practice can be seen utmost at a glance if .your readers will get White Paper (No. 119), which deals with Urban 'Districts (Areas and Rates)." This is a Return I%lr. Outhwaite got from the Local Government Board. It is, in its way, one of the severest in- dictments of the present rating system that has ever been published. Let, me just mention -a few great towns, to show the 'state of affairs. Take Manchester. There are over 21,000 acres; over 5,000 acres the rated as "agricultural " land, which contribute only £1,611 out of £1,759,000. Liverpool is little better. 'There are 19,500 acres, of which over 3,800 are rated as " agricultural " land, contributing only £1,500 out of over £1,598,000. Newcastle-Upon- Tyne is proportionately as bad as any great, town. There are over 8,700 acres, of which 2,290 are rated as "agricultural" land contri- buting only £542 out of £484,587. The instances of preposterous inequalities could be .given by the -column from this White Paper, but to: .those interested in the question it is only neces- sary for them to spend .3d. and study it for , themselves. NOw the Report of the 1885 .Royal Commission, together-with this. Local Govern- merit Board paper, should be sufficient to con- vince anyone engaged in industry in this coun- try that a: change in. our rating system and a flat tax on all land values in town and country are not only urgent, but are essential for the true fulfilment of Ministers' pledges. ' No land campaign based on mere tinkering with the housingiquestion, dealing with mini- mum wage, small holdings, and land courts, will be satisfactory to the vast mass of the people of this country.' • The agricultural labourers know from experience that economic salvation is not to .be gained from a minimum wage. The people who live under conditions of squalor and vile sanitation know that the reform of our housing question on lines of buy- ing bits of land and taxing: the many for the benefit of the few, leaving land monopoly where it Is, will be ineffective for the future welfare of our people. The Men and women of this generation in town and country are sick and tired of palliatives. They are weary witif waiting for Governments to carry out their pledges, and only real land reform of an economic nature which will lift the burdens of tax and rate .from industry, and place them on those values which are created by the comma-' nity. will ensure the return at the next Election of the Liberal Government.

A NEGLECTED INJURY. Death Caused Through Paying No

Attention to a Wound.

BLOOD POISONING INTERVENES.

At the Warrington Coroner's Court on Satur- day, Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest on the body of Richard Bailey (3(i), of Canal Side Cot- tages, Grappenhall, who died from blood poisoning in the Infirmary the previous Thurs- day. The following gentlemen attended: Mr. H. E. Brothers (H.M. Inspector of Factories), Mr. E. A. Connor (relatives), and Mr. R. M. Monk (for the firm). Mrs. Rose Bailey (widow) said that her hus- band was employed as a steel roller maker at Messrs. Robertson's steel works, Latchford. He

had 'always been a fairly healthy man, and during the four years she had been married to him he had never been ill. On Friday, the sth inst., he showed her a cut on the second finger of the left hand. It looked as though it had been cut with a knife. Deceased said he had received it at his work. He put a kid glove finger on it, and took no notice of it until Sun- day morning, when he complained that it was very sore. It was very swollen and she (witness) put a linseed poultice on it and poulticed it at interVals, throughout the ,day. On the' Monday morning deceased said he had a lump under his left arm. Dr. Jago was called in and told her to continue the poulticing and for her husband to call and see him on Wednesday. Deceased was too ill to go, and the doctor ordered his removal to the Infirmary, where he died on Thursday. He had not given her any details of the accident.

Wrapped in a Dirty Rag. James Wright, steel roller maker, nose Villa, Latchford Without, said that when deceased out his finger they were working on lathes. Deceased said "Have you cut your thumb?" and he (witness) said that he had. Deceased then showed him the finger he had mit. It was only a small cut and deceased wrapped a piece- of dirty rag round it and took no notice of it. He worked up to the Saturday and never said anything about the cut. He saw Mrs. Bailey on Sunday and she informed him that her husband had a lump under his arm. In answer to the Coroner, witness said that one of the spiral shavings from a steel bar had caused the cut. _

The Inspector: Have you antiseptic dressings at the works?—Yes; in the time office.

Mr. Monks: That Is so

The Cordner: They seldom come, I suppose.

Clear Case of Blood Poisoning.

_ Mr. Monk: It depends on the nature of the Injury. If a man considers it serious he comes. If the case is considered above the ebility of the dresser the injared man .is sent ko a doctor. .We did not know this man had been hurt. We express our sincere sympathy with the rela- tives.

The Coroner (to Wright): Was the cut you had larger than deceased's? Witness: Yes; it took good ways, as it happened. Detective-Sergeant Holland said tie could hardly see the cut. It was stated that the firm did not dispute that death was due to blood poisoning, but they might dispute that it happened at work. Dr. Law (senior house surgeon at the Infir- mary) said it was a clear case of blood poison- ing.

;Examined, he said: I could not say unhesi- tatingly that blood poisoning a-rose from the Wound, but the presumption is that it camp from That, as it was the only cut deceased had on the fingers.

The Coroner: Would it he difficult to account Tor ,the blood poisoning otherwise than by the ffnuer? . Witness: it would.

'The jury returned a verdict of "Accidents death," and said that blood poisoning was se up by the cut on the finger.

THE HEALING VIRTUES OF HERBS.

Ancient Remeales Again the Fashion.

ANOTHER WONDERFUL DISCOVERY.

The cultivation of herbs for medicinal pur- poses has received marked attention recently, and the virtues of herbal remedies are once more being widely acknowledged. The dandelion as a liver cleanser; the com- mon little wood sorrel as a blood purifier; the bitter camomile as a tonic, are everyday examples. There is a safety and reliability in herbal medicinss that does not ' belong to mineral drugs. In the case of Zam-Buk, we find our most wonderful 'exa,inple of herbal compounding. The rarest and costliest herbs have to be found to make this grand balm; its manufactfire calls for the finest skill and machinery money can buy. The processes of refinement, blending and maturing require a period of three months to finally complete. Such a 'wonderfully complex substance is Zam-Buk that the cleverest analysts in the world have been beaten in their efforts to probe the secret of its composition. Zam-buk contains those herbal substances which Nature has intended for the use of man ever since she bequeathed to him the instinct to rub a place that hurts.

Apart from the fact that it is conceived on altogether novel lines, Zam-Buk contains none of the animal fats which are present in ordinary ointments and which cause these ointments to turn rancid and useless in a short time. By reason of its rare herbal origin, Zam-Buk "keeps " for all time, and this alone is a reason why every home should keep a box of Zam-Buk always handy, especially where there are children. Whether it is a bruise that threatens to fester, a patch of eczema that irritates and burns, pimples that disfigure, or an attack of piles, Zam-Buk is the one ideal herbal healer that is superior to all ordinary medicine.

the Graze as a Serious Evil.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME IMPORTANT RECOMMENDATIONS.

Some members of the 'Warrington Education Committee are of the opinion that no child should be allowed to attend cinematograph shows until 14 years of age. It was said that children sent home from school owing to disease attended, these places of amusement, and thus assisted to spread the disease. The Committee recommended better ventilation and more sunlight in the picture theatres; a minimum. limit of distance between the audi- ence and the pictures; and the exclusion of children dUring school hours.

The question of children and cinematograph shows was discussed at the meeting of the Warrington Education Committee held in the Town Hall on Monday evening. Councillor H. Roberts presided. The discussion arose upon the following resolution from the Schbol Man- agement Committee : " That in the interests of public health, as well as in the educational interests of the schobl children, the • Health Committee of the Town Council be invited to consider whether it is not desirable for steps to be taken to provide for (1) the adequate ventilation of the premises dur- ing performances and the efficient admission of sunlight into the premises when no perform- ance is taking place; (2) a fixed minimum limit of distance between the audience and the pic- tures; and (3) the exclusion of children of school age from performances during school hours." Mr. H. KINGDON said he had had no oppor- tunity of discussing the matter,. or of finding out the views of the committee, but he thought the time would come, if it had not come already, when they would have to take,action in regard to the cinematographic shows even more dras- tically than that resolution proposed. Most of us, he continued, have seen these shows--. 1 plead guilty that I have and that I enjoyed ihem—(A Voice: "Shame ")—and I think most of us cannot help feeling, even when beautiful pictures are thrown upon the screen, there is a great deal that is not desirable for young children to see.

of judging what is really the, best for them. Some of those gentlemen :experts seem to have been cited. / plead guilty of going to Picture shows, and I have not seen anything wrong. Perhaps I have been to the wrong shows and saw nothing that is demoralising.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
A DRASTIC PROPOSAL.

I should like to see a recommendation that no child, while attending school, should be allowed to attend 'cinematograph entertain- ments. Whether this is practical or not, it is for the committee to decide. 'There is no ques- tion that what you 'say about the vibration is true, though that'can be altered. There is also the. question of the danger to the eyesight by the vibration, but in' the majority of the shows this has been reduced to a minimum, and it might be improved still more. But we have to consider it 'apart the moral point of view. Let us consider it from the health point of view. ,Here we as a. Medical Inspection Com- mittee' have the duty ,of 'saying that children Suffering from certain defects shall not attend school. There Is nothing to :prevent them going to the picture tadaces, and a great many .of. them do .so. There you have children crowded together,' SOMe-being-the children ex- cluded from school on account of -Sonia defeet, and these- children spread the di:Seas°.

LIBERAL OPEN-AIR MEETING.

Mr. J. B. Martindale, the prospective Liberal candidate, and Mr. F. Neilson, M.P. for Hyde, were the speakers at a great open-air demon- stration held at Widnes on:Saturday. Mr. Martindale, speaking about the suggested conference on Heine Rule, said that after the Liberal party had been twice returned to powe: and the Tory party had been given an oppor- tunity of presenting their alternatives, he was of opinion that the people of the country had made up their minds on the question. The only effect a conference could have would be to ,damp the aspirations of the Irish people and lead the EngHai electorate to believe that their opinions could not he reflected through Parlia- ment.

Mr. Neilson, 31. P., spoke on the land question, r.nd said that the present system penalised in- dustry, and land in the town was held up, so that larger prices could be obtained.

A CURATE SAYS FAREWELL, '4

The Rev. C. M. Chavasse, who is leaving St. Helens Varian Church, after working with re- markible success for four years as curate, gave his farewell address on Sunday to the Men's Bible Class. He said that the class had proved a great means for good in the town.

A HISTORIC CHURCH ORGAN.

The historic organ of Holy Cross Catholic Church, St. helens, was re-opened on Sunday, after rebuilding operations. The organ ,was -built originally for, the great Exhibition of '1862, and it was transferred to St., Helens, where for fifty years it was in. the, capable hands Of the late 'Mr. Unsworth, • The latest tubular pneu- matic action has now .been 'adopted, and a new Vox Celeste stop of fofty-six notes added.

"ALL A PACK OF LIES."

- James W. Bunting, shoemaker, South More- land-street, was charged, at St. Helens on Sat- urday, with obtaining food and lodging by false pretences. The prisoner arranged with a Mrs. Trehearne to be a lodger at her house in Emily- street, Thatto Heath, saying his father was chief sanitary inspector of London, and that he had comA to serve under the St. Helens Corporation as an inspector.. He stayed there three days, when it was found out that his story was quite untrue. Prisoner now said that the story was all a pack. of lies, and he was committed for trial at Liverpool Quarter Sessions.

VIOLENT MINERS.

Thomas Kelly, collier, 10, Marsh-street, was fined 20s. and costs, at St. Helens on Saturday, for assaulting Constable Goold, and was ordered to find sureties for good behaviour, or go to prison for one month. A Polish miner, John Roscovitch, Bewsey- street, Thatto Heath, was ordered to find sure- ties to be of good' behaviour by the St. Helens magistrates on Saturday. Sergeant Stewart said he had smashed all the furniture, including the pictures, and was dancing on the splinters. He had threatened to murder his wife, and was about to set fire to the house.

Sequel to Early Morning Removal.

Wright (in the chair) and other magistrates, Eliza Vrince, now of Hereford, was charged with fraudulently removing goods to avoid distraint for Tent, and James H. Prince was charged with aiding and abetting. The complainant was Dr. Fox. Mr. Bradley repre- sented the latter, and Mr. A. Browne defended.

Shortly before the case came on the woman fainted in the dock and was attended below.' She did not appear throughout the case. When the charges had been read out, Mr. Browne said: "We indignantly deny it." The Clerk explained that the Carrii had been partly heard in August. Mr. Bradley said that the defendant occupied the house, 51, Wash-lane, Latchford, on_a quar- terly tenancy of £l4 per year. Several attempts had been made to distrain, but it was impossible to effect an entrance. The furniture would be worth ..tlO, and he asked the magistrates to make an order for £l4, the actual rent due.

Robert Lowe said he was the estate agent for Dr. Pox. He had witnessed the tenancy agree- ment (produced), and the house was let to Mrs. Prince. When defendant took the premises he erected a shed, where he worked as a tool maker.

Mr. Shakeshaft had the shed afterwardst—l have never seen Mr. Shakeshaft. Mr. Browne: Defendant will swear that he arranged with Dr. Fox that the shed should be for the rent, and that he told the doctor he was leaving.

By Mr. Bradley: The rent would be due from Mrs. Prince.

David Morris, 93, Slater-street, said he was employed by Mr. J. Edwards as a carter. Ile went to the house between one o'clock and two o'clock in the morning. It took him abmit an hour to load. He took the goods to Wrexham, where he arrived at six o'clock at night

An Early Start. Jack Edwards, carter, said he arranged with Mr. Prince on the night previous to the re- moval.

SEQUEL TO A LOAN.

Ends in the Police Court.

A loan and a squabble over the repayment had a sequel at the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, when, before Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair) and other magistrates, James Lee, Howley-lane, Warrington, was charged with assaulting Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Thomas Top- ham, tine Mrs. Mary Lee was charged with assaulting Mrs. 'Popham. Joseph T. Topham, 7, Howley-lane, said that at 11 o'clock on Saturday night he was asleep on the couch when Lee came into the house. [Prisoner hit witness in the stomach, and' witness ' asked, "What have you done that for; what brought you in?" Prisoner said "My legs." He again struck at witness, but fell, and he (Top- ham) went upstairs, but the prisoner followed, and threw him on the bed, where Mrs. Topham was sleeping. She got up' and, on going down- stairs, Mrs. Lee assaulted her. The Chief Constable: Is there any debt be- tween you. Witness: There is something between my wife and Mrs. Lee. • Prisoner: The door was open and he dragged me in the house and locked the door. • They were both drunk. I never struck him at all. He knocked me to the floor. The bother is because they owe us 155., and won't pay it. Mrs. Topham said that Lee gave her two smacks on the shoulders as she lay in bed. Mrs. Lee met her at the bottom of the stairs and hit her over the eye with a clog. Her legs were black and blue and she had to be taken to .the liafirinarY.- Her headas covered with bandages. Mrs. Lee: You were both drunk to the world. I. had no clogs on. , . . Mrs. Topham: I was not drunk Mrs: Lee: You are a nuisance to the street, for you are " carryint on" every night. You were " speechless " drunk, and you said, "If I cannot take it out of one, I will take it out of ' the other." Detective-Sergeant Reddington said that when they executed the warrant prisoner was in the Gladstone Arms, Church-street. In reply to he warrant he said, "I went.to ask for 15s. that Mrs. Tepham owes my wife. They pulled me inside and Topham struck me, and I hit him in self-defence." The woman said, "I pulled Mrs. Topham away to prevent her biting my husband's legs." Lee:, He said he would sooner do a month than pay us a halfpenny. Mrs. Lee: Mrs. Topha.m was .biting my hue- bind on the legs. She never buys anything. She begs soap, soda, blue, butter, sugar and tea. She is drunk from Monday. to Sunday. • Lee's son said that Topham struck •Lee on the hand with a poker. The Chief Constable: Prisoners were here 12 months ago for a similar offence. They are terrors to the neighbourhood, and although I do not sug- gest that complainants are angels, they want protecting from such assaults. Lee has been 21 times•previously and the woman makes her 11th appearance. She has been five times before the Court Rix- common assaults. Lee was fined 20s. and costs, or one month's imprisonment, and, in view of her delicate state of health, the case against Mrs. Lee was dismissed.

A MERCILESS JUDGE.

A merciless judge is Father Time. Before him the weak and the wanting go to the wall. Only, the truth can stand. For years the follow- ing statement from an Earlestown resident has withstood this sternest of all tests.

NEARLY - TWO YEARS AGO, Mrs. M.-haste, of 232, Earle-street, Earlestown, said: "After my last confinement kidney complaint crept upon me. I, gradually got worse, and about five years ago dropsy set in. My body filled with water, and I had swellings all over me. There were watery swellings under my eyes, and my back caused me fearful pain. HI stooped the pain was un- bearable; it seemed as if a sharp knife were being driven into my back. I used to come over so dizzy that I dared not go about. My head ached fit to burst. - -

"I went to the Liverpool Royal Infirmary, whero I was told I should have to undergo an operation. I refused, however, and started to uso Doan's backache kidney .pills, which I had been advised to try. I noticed an improvement after the first box or so, so I took the pills regu- larly, determined to give them a trial. As I continued with the medicine all my aches and pains left me, the swellings gradually disap- peared, and I became my normal size again. Now I feel I am quite cured, and I can honestly say Doan's pills have made a new woman of me. I shall always praise the medicine, and I hope my experience with Doan's pills will induce others to try this excellent remedy., (Signed) 'Mrs. M. Haste." NEARLY TWO YEARS LATER, Mrs. Haste said: "I am delighted to 'say that I am keeping 'champion.' Doan's backache kidney pills made a wonderful cure in my case." WILLIAM BEAMONT. Few men deserved better of his town and townspeople than did the late William Beamont and the ,Warrington Society will have justified its existence by the simple act they performed yesterdity of placing a- tablet on the building in which he worked as a solicitor for fifty years. The tablet draws attention, to a per§an- ality of which- Warrington .will always be proud.

WASTED BRAIN POWER.

We direct attention to our article in which 'pointed attention is drawn to The fact that Warrington has-Ac.tioscholarships .to the Univer- sities by which boys and girls with brains, but insufficient money behind them, are brought to a stand by a handicap that ought % not to exist. The brains of the community belong to the community, ,and the best ought not to be wasted for lack. of means to proceed. We occupy a position Of splendid isolation: as a county, but we suffer for it in some respects. If we are a county we must do as counties do, and, not penalise a brilliant student because he or she has the misfortune to live within our boundaries. •

conservative State in the world, to venerate themselves as men.

AN-OLD WARRINGTON BUSINESS.

From an advertisement which appears in another column, it will be seen that our con- tributor, Mr. A. Mounfield, has succeeded to the old business of Joseph Mountield arra Sons. [hose who may have feared the cessation of Ills articles as a result of this new responsi-

dlity, may be reassured. With a true journal- istic faculty for turning everything into grist for the mill, he this week gives the ,contenls of some old papers rescued, we presume, from the archives of the old firm. .

We ourselves have just a little sentiment about old businesses, and always deplore the cessation of old firms, which have Won repute by. generations of good work and faithful deal- ing. We are glad to think that Mr. Mounfield has stepped in to perpetuate Ifiis .particular undertaking. Warrington has several ,Which are reminiscent of the long ago, and 'of' them, the firm of Joseph Mounfield and Sons is one. Possibly, in point of age "Shaw, Butcher" (we quote. the familiar designation) would- hold the pre-eniinenee. In point of, a business held in direct descent (we speak. subject to correction), the firm of Carter and Son,, bakers, might be first, but in any list J. Mounfield and Sons would hold a very high place, as it has held a high place in the esteem of the town for sound work and reasonable dealing.

WHAT WARRINGTON NEEDS.

We have received many congratulatory mes- sages upon bur last -week's , article 'calling for the abatement of the Snioke nuisanee in the interests of the publid health and general amenities of the town. What has called for most praise is the fact that our inquiry in the output of smoke was carried out in a thorough and scientific mtinner, and that the whole question was clean .with in a judicious and moderate fashion. We have heard some., ex- pressions of opinion- that have not been favour- able to our efforts, but as we said last week, all that is asked for is that the smoke of the town Should be reduced to its minimum. The aboll7 titon of smoke is an impossibility, but to reduce a appreciably is possible, and it is the duty of the _Health Committee to see that it is so re- duced. On this point we are assured by a practical man—a Master who knows as much about iton works conditions as most men—that nine-tenths of the smoke that now blackens our pskies and pollutes the air by which we live, could be avoided. If nine-teliths of the plague were removed a could willingly bear with the remaining tenth. That inevitable :remainder would do little harm—it is the superfluous out- put against which we protest, and against which we ask the authorities ,to protect the community. For instance, the Corporation might put their own house in order, and so clear the way for approaching, with a clear'cOn- science, other delinquents. There is a very bad offender owned by, the 'Corporation, that was miSsed by the inspector whose report we pub- lished- last 'week, but it is well known to the members of the Council. Once the electricity works chimney was a great offender, but mechanical stokers have Made the chimney one of the most harmless erections in flue .land. What has ;been, done at the etectrieity works can be done elsewhere, and we must all insist upon its being done. Insistence ought riot to be necessary, for experience teaches that the throwing off of smoke is equivalent to rfirewing gold dust to th Winds.. ' -Writing on "What Warrington Needs," a correspondent ,says:---.

One Of the things.is certainly a jolly good wakingcliP on the :abominable smoke nuis- ance question, and I" was very .glad indeed to see. in your paper last Saturday such a clever and, I hope, useful page of informa- tion. If only you Could impress upon the housewivep .of the town the time, labour and money tte'y would 'save by getting the smoke redn'&ed, it might help towards a Beautiful Warrington, but as long as chim- neys are a)lowed to smoke as stated in de- tails of observation, the Beautiful Warring- ton Society may as well devote their ener- gies towards something else.
