A WeINDERFUL RECORD.

The annual meeting of the local branch of the British and*oreign Bible Society will take place ,next week, in the most encouraging cir- cumstances. Never before has the society been able to discharge better its duty towards Chris-. tendom than during the twelve, months that have, just gonenever" before have more copies Of, the Scriptures been sent into the world to call people to that better sort of introspection which opens out possibilities on the sacred and higher planes of life. It would .be tedious to speak of the millions of copies of the Scriptures that have been showered broadcast over 'the earth by tne Society through a century of hard and .ofttimes discouraging labour. The num- bers involved are so vast as to make it impos- sible for thd human mind to grasp their import, but the number increases at an increasing rate, last year's 'figure of 7,899,000 copies being, an increase of half; a million on the year. Some idea of the, determination with which the Society have approached the task of reaching all peoples 'may- be gatheted from the .simple statement that they have .now issued the Com- plete Bible in .111 different language's, the New Testament in an additional 108 languages, and at least. one I3ook of the Scriptures in 231 Other languages. *lt is a-inarvellous-record.that May Wel claim credit; "as It'"dOes,'for having been -a factor in bringing about some of the remark- able changes, social arid 'political; that-have been witnessed in many. parts of the world. 'Unprecedented .activities have happily been accompanied by 'unprecedented support, last year's income having passed the revenue in any previous normal year.

THE WORK OF THE BIBLE.

What part have the Bible and the work of the Society played in the movement that has shaken up the sleepy East in such a surprising manner within the last few. years?. This ques- tion is suggested in the literature issued by the Society. Revolt. against the old bad conditien of things - has been frequent—witness the strange changes in Japan, China, Persia and the Nearer East.

To what extent the work of the British and Foreign Bible Society has been raspon- siblt,for this great awakening of the world, and for the rousing of the whole human race to realise the higher possibilities of which life, both individual and nation'al, is capable; it is imposaible, to estiMate... mit it is a, fact that .all over the,warld men are beginning to read, and .it is to 'the Bible - that; they are chiefly turning. As the result of the society's work among . the nations, peoples and races, the Bible, of all, books', is the most widely read. In many languages, it is the only printed book in existence. The apparent connection between the two— the renaissance and the turning of the peoples in restless ominous fashion—reminds one forcibly of the time in our own. history when the Bible became the one book read by the Eng- lish people, a habit which produced the Puritan, gave him a language, directed his every action, and so laid the foundations of modern Britain. We can imagine, how New Testament enlight- enment will make the Orientals burst the bonds which, worse than our own paralysing feudal- ism, have held them in their fatal grip for ages. If the Bible has helped them to start upon the road to freedom, it has once more proved' its ability tO establish that Heaven upon earth, upon which the Puritans had their eye, and which they succeeded in establishing more firmly than some scoffers are *illing to admit. In England We shall soop rank men higher than gold, and flesh and blood before property; we are glad to think that a non-party organisation like the British and Foreign Bible Society see tlie day coining when increasing intelligence will force the peoples, even of China, the most

DO IT NOW.

11 you have, a kind word—say it, Throbbing hearts soon sink to rest: If you owe a kindness—Ray it, Life's sun hurries to the West.

Can you do a kind deed—do it, From despair some soul to save; Bless each day as you pass through it. Marching onward to the grave.

Days for deeds are few, my brother, Then to day fulfil your vow ; If yon mean to help another, Do not dream it—do it now.

The Speechless Spoke. . • We always thought that if anyone were " speechless" drunk that Person Was tempor- arily dumb. According to a prisoner at the Police Court this week, a woman whom she alleged was " Speechless " drunk, said "If I cannot take it out of one, I will take it out of the other." Why Is It?

At a flower show in Scotland a gentleman said tfiat the Highland fling always made him think of a kilted Highlander dancing among thistles and trying to save his legs as much as possible. It is curious, says the " Tatler," the vay the thoughts of Scotchmen continually run to thistles.

Money and Foolishness. What one might term a public benefactor was brought before-the local magistrates on Tuesday. The officer proving a. charge of drunknness said he was stopping people and giving them money for drink, Prisoner: I was giving my son a copper. Officer: He had stopped another man before his son.

The Chief suggested that prisoner give an undertaking not to be so " foolish," and the case was dismissed.

Slightly Mixed.

Bieisell, the oldest member, and' honorary superintendent of the Sabbath school, spoke of the services Mrs. Wright rendered as a teacher in the school, at the opening services, and immediately following that time, which went back about forty years.—The Rev. A. J. Chessum also spoke.,

PLUCKY LADY SWIMMER. A great attraction at the Warrington Swim- ming Club Gala on Thursday should be The ex- hibition to be given by Iliss Louie Wilkinson, of Preston. This is 'the plucky lady, who, on the 4th of September, swam from Preston to Lytham, a distance of nine miles. When two miles from the end of the swim she Was seized with cramp, but persisted in continuing, and completed the task with one leg. There are other star attractions at. the gala, anti it (flight to draw a bumper house.

- WARRINGTON SMOKE. We are glad to learn that the l'own Council, especially the Health .Coannittee, is sericiusly considering the smoke question'. No on-, will be better pleased than ourselves if the Health Committee assumes and discharges its duties with thoroughness. We have no wish—it would be no pleasure to us--to be continually tilting against the Health Committee, composed, as it is, of citizens who have in other ways rendered useful public service. But we want to make it clear that in this matter we have a duty to the suffering, public, and we intend to strive to discharge this duty, -without' respect to persOn or party. We are quite ready to let the matter rest if we see evidence that the Health Com- mittee realises its duty and is taking steps to discharge it. But any action must be con- tinuous, systematic, and effective. The public will not be satisfied with erratic action, designed to lull to sleep what may be considered a passing spasm of public activity. There must be nothing of a wind Ow-dressing character in- tended to make a show and to form material for a page in the annual health report. W t the public look for is an effective carrying Out of the law, designed to protect their interest and placed in the hands of the Town Councit to

enforce. The action Of the Health Committee will be judged, rightly judged, and judged only, by its effectiveness in restraining the emissions of .black smoke within the limits of the 'law. No member of the Health Committee can pit for- ward-the claim' that the committee hes carried out the law and protected the public effectively, beoause every day and every hour the emis- sions from the 'power-produc.ing,, plants .in the town contradicts such a claim.. We would emphasise the necessityof public" policy. being confinuotts -on behalf of' the manufacturer, as as of the .public.. .The mannfacturer has not hitherto known-what the Health Committee deinands, or, rather, what thq Couneil, is pre- pared to enforce. Once the Council estab- lished the fact that their regulation Omit of five minutes' plack smoke in tin half-hour, or two minutes' contintions, is local law, the manufac- turers would know to what ,they had to con- form,. and act accordingly. It' has been the uncertainty that has rendered practically use- less the feeble remonstrances which have occa- sionally emanated from the Health Committee and introduced the personal factor. Let it be understood thlat this is not a question as to who happens to be mayor, or what. the constitution of, the Health Committee; but that it is the settled law of the Borough-of Warring- ton, which will be enforced as consilently and tfirmly as a regulation' about fin ashpit. No property-Owner plinks any the worse of Coun- cillor A. because .he is on the committee' that orders him to clean a. blocked drain. ,con, tinuous enforcement -of the law would remove that personal factor. which, it is thought, is at the back of the minds of several lnembers, and keeps them in chronic fear of ffending some powerful moneyed interest, and at the same time leaves the manufacturer in ignorance .of that to Which he has to conform. We should like to see the electors apply a healthy stimulus by making this question of the•purification of the air we breathe a plank in. the approaching municipal election pliftfOrm.

and her Work as a Teacher, A CHERISHED MEMORY.

"That this committee .receive with great regret the intimation of Miss Corney's death, and request the Direcror of Education to con- vey to her parents and relatives an expression of the committee's sympathy with them in their bereav,ement ; that they, be further informed Of the. eOninfttee's apprechition of the good work clone by Miss Corney in .the education of and .general care for the young children who came under her influence, and of their deep sense of the loss they have sustained by the early ter- mination of so bright .and promising a career."

Such was the resolution solemnly and silently adopted at the meeting, of the Warrington 'Education Committee, 'lick! on Monday night, in respect to the death of Miss Marie Louise Corney, the highly esteemed head .mistress of the Sacred Heart School, Bank Quay. Councillor Roberts, who moved the resolu- tion, paid a mark .od tribute to the work of the late head mistress. Miss Corney, he said, had beep 'eon!! ec ted with the_ Sacred Heart. School, as assistant and head'inWres,' for. seven years, and he was not exaggerating when he -sad. that no member of. the ,committee's teaChirig staff hat been more acceptable to the scholars And parents, or to lifost generally, interested in the School 4DfwhiclP'she had charge. •- __

Sb had, died at a .very early age,,' but had left behind her a memory, Which would long ebe cherished; of many acts of kindness.. She re- tained up to the last the affectionate regardiof fill associated with her and the love of the chil- dren entrusted to-her oversight.

THE FUNERAL

The funeral took place at the Cemetery, but prior to •the interment an impressive 'service was held in St. Mary's Church, where there •W(IS ft large congregation, gathered to show their last respect ;to one who was a highly es- teemed teachei- and Wend. WASTED BRAINS.

How Our Intelligent Boys and Girls are Handicapped.

NEGLECTFUL WARRINGTON.

A question of supreme importance to Warring. ton is raised by a letter we have this week received, and reproduce below. The letter, evidently written by a student, who feels acutely the need for the establishment of Warrington scholarships to the universities, makes some pointed comparisons between the facilities that are given by other education authorities, and the entire absence of facilities afforded locally.

BRAINS BUT NO SCHOLARSHIPS.

TO THE EDITOR OF "TIE EXAMINER."

do not think that there has been any suggestion Of a University Scholarship for Secondary School pupils to' any* of the North- ern Universities. This is sorely needed. Could not some of, .the wealthy gentlemen of the town,' or even the Education Committee, give some sort of incentive to an ambitions scholar desirous of entering a University, but being unable to do so on account of the expense? Comparing with neighbouring Lancashire towns and cities—to the Manchester University, Manchester Education Committee has seven scholarships; Oldham, 5 (£5O each) ; Burnley, ,Bolton, 3; Salford, 1 (value £35). Widnes Secondary School has 'several to' Liverpool. The pupil living outside ,the boundary h-as a better chance than, the town scholar, since he or She can compete in a Lancashire or Cheshire County Council scholarship (about 15), for which a persoa residing in the town is in- eligible. The Grammar School has one or two to Cam- bridge. Why cannot the Secondary School have one to either Liverpool or Manchester? A tutor. at Manchester University has asked this question from several students (from War- rington) about to enter the University.. The successes of former students at the scbool show that Warrington can compete favourably with other towns as regards "brains."

It appears to me that the wrong institutions and the wrong persons are- benefiting by the high rates. If expenses were cut down in an- ther, direction, surely, Warrington could afford sc to give at least one. holarship for Secondary School youths. I, for one, am sure that a scholarship would be enthusiastically welcomed both by teachers and scholars; the only pity is that it is so late.

ARDINES -AD SOLEMe

WHAT MIGHT BE DONE.

There are at the present moment at least four brilliantly equipped lads at me econuary School—all passed the Northern University Matriculation or equivalent examinations, and all are at a blind alley unless they enter the teaching profession or the Civil Service. One or them would, with help .to the University, blossom into a ,physicist of eminence, such is his 'promise; another, a ghemist of reputation, and a_ third, a fine dialectitian, and so on. *Three .years' fees paid by .the Education Corn- mittee would frank these three lads on their passage tb a career, an honours degree would follow as sure as Warrington smoke. The rest could safely be left in their, keeping—they are as ambitions as they are clever, and as indus- trious as they are successful. Two scholarships this' year of £5O for three years, two additional ones next, and two each of the following years, will: 'never mean More than £.3oo_a :year, when the system is in full working order, and the town, with its thriving industries, which benefit so much from scien- tific research, can well afford it. Some parents would accept the payment of fees tifone, and would be glad to continue the sustentation ,of their child. This would allow of elastiOity in the scheme, and give opportunity for additional help where it was more. greatly needed. The material is to hand, thanks to the ex- cellent work dine at the Secondary School; the money is to hand, for the COmmittee can easily afford the cost, and ,every Warrington child should have as rosy an educational future as its 'Cheshire and Lancashire neighbour.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT OTHERS ARE DOING.

A casual glance at a daily newspaper will show how others are leaving us behind in. this vital educational race. In one day's paper this week we found the following significant an- nouncements

Cheshire Scholarship Awards.

At a meeting of the Cheshire County Educa- tion Committee yesterday the following univer- sity scholarships were awarded: Dorothy. Spencer, of Sale, Holloway College, London.; George F. Seel, of Macclesfield, Corpus Christi College, Oxford; Roy Mellor, of Macclesfield, Manchester University ; 'Gertrude F. Cocks, of Manchester, Girton College; Charles 'Croxson, of Crewe, Mary B. Gifford, of Macclesfield, Frederick V. Tideswell, of Crewe, and Hilda Burgess, of Stockport, scholarships tenable at Manchester University. A county university scholarship in agriculture and horticultum was awarded to James Harrison, of Bebington. It

was notified to the Committee that a national scholarship tenable at 'the Royal College of Art had been'awarded to Stanley B. Potter, of -the Macclesfield Sehool- of Art.

A Father's Appreciation.

The Chairman at the meeting of the Man- chester Education Committee said he had re- ceiVe,d a long letter from a father thanking the Committee for the opportunities provided by the scholarship scheme, whereby four of his sons had been able to enjoy the advantages of secondary arid university education, which, owing to lack of means, would otherwise have been impossible. The two eldest boys in the family referred to won science and art scholar- ships at the secondary school and later pro- ceeded to the School of Technology with uni- versity scholarships. They both obtained their degree and now occupied good positions as head chemists in large works. The third, a daughter, is pursuing the same course with a university scholarship awarded by' the Com- mittee, and another son, who held a junior secondary school scholarship has fol- lowed the example of his brothers and sister by gaining a university scholarship at the recent examinations. These successe3 '''deserve - notice, not merely for the ability displayed''by four mem- bers of the same family, but as showing the value of the Committee's scheme to a parent of limited means. In a letter -written fo him by the father of these clever children, he wrote: "I trust you will pardon me for troubling you with this long letter, but I felt that I could not do less than write to thank you and the EduCa- tion Committee for giving four of my children both a secondary and a university educa- tion which my own very limited means would never have enabled me to do."

"EDUCATION AND PROGRESS." An interesting lecture will be delivered under the auspices of the Warrington Adult School Sub-union in the School, Academy-street. on. Saturday next. by Professor B. Moore, of Liver- pool University. His subject will be "Educa- tion and Progress."

. PROGRESSIVE LABOUR LEAGUE. Mr. T. IL Garside, Manchester, will again speak At the Bridge Foot meeting of the Pro- gressive Labour League on Monday. He doubt- less will have something important and inter- esting to say.

CLEARANCE SALE OF CYCLES.—AII prices reduced. —4. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge-street, War- rington.

Died on Eve of Holiday.

111iss .Marie- Cabill (71), Cambrian-view, Ches- tr, met with a tragic death when about to visit 60nie Warrington friends. She was engaged Packing her box, when a bed near caught fire, and the old lady was suffocated by the smoke. The jury's verdict was "Accidental death."

The Population Increased.

Through a larger number of births being I'ecorded last week the population of Warrington Was increased by 17. The births numbered 31 120 males, 11 females), and of these three females Were illegitimate. The birth-rate was 23.9, com- pared with 31.2 for the same week last year.

Beer by Motor Lurry.

Beer had to be ensured for the people of Liver- pool even if there was a railwaar.strike in pro- gress. To Meet their deMaiids horse and motor lurries this week were kept running from War- rington almost in processional order. The beer, in times of peace, is usually sent by rail.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
Effects of the Strikes.

The effect of the dock strike at Manchester and the railway strike at Liverpool has seen the passage through Warrington this week of an abnormal amount of heavy motor traffic. The police on point duty at the crossing at Sankey and Buttermarket-streets have experienced a busy time.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
Motor Cars Mixed Up.

A collision occurred between two motor cars at the corner of Sankey-street and Crosfield- street on Monday night. The cars were driven. by Peter Clowe, Chorlton-on-Medlock, Manches- ter, and Robert Plinston, 104, Bridge-street, and they collided with considerable force. The occu- pants escaped unhurt, but both vehicles were badly damaged.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
Slimmer Scourge Decreasing.

The deaths from summer diarrhcea in War- rington last week were five, compared with nine the previous week. The advent of cooler weather is responsible for the decrease. The deaths numbered 17 (12 males, 5 females), giving a death-rate of 11.9, compared with 13.5 for the same week last year. Six new cases of scarlet fever have been reported.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
Baths for a School.

At Warrington EdAcation Committee on,Mon- day a scheme wai submitted for accommodating a boiler to lent the baths at Evelyn-street School. Councillor Pemberton objected to the matter being rushed through. The members were entitled to details. Mr. R. Fairclough said the present accommodation was too small for the boiler. The new building was therefore necessary.—The Chairman: It is an addition to the original plan?—Mr. Fairclough : Yes.—Mr. Jackson Holmes: But there will be a consider- able set-off against the expenditure.—The scheme was adopted.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
Co-operative Union Choir Contest.

The annual choir contest of the Co-operative Union takes place in the Parr Hall on October 4th. when most of the leading choirs of Lan- cashire and Yorkshire will compete. The selected pieces are Challinor's "The Nights," and Bantock's "On Himalay." Dr. Henry Coward, the well-known musician, will be the judge. In the evening the choirs will give a first-class concert, and at this Mr. J. Upson will nreside and an address will be given by Mr. W. Gregory. Next Saturday, September 27th, an exhibition of Co-operative nroductisns will open in the Empire Skating Rink.

Presentation to Mr. G. F. Freeman. An interesting presentation took place at the office of Messrs. T. and J. L. Tunstall and Co., Bewsey Chambers, Warrington, on Wednesday, Mr. G. F. Freeman being the recipient of a hand- some gold watch chain, on the occasion of his retirement from the firm, in whose employ he has been for the past 22 years, to take up his new duties as licensee of the "Bull's Head," Church- street. Mr. Tunstall, in making the presenta- tion, commented upon the .valuable services rendered to the krm by Mr. Freeman, and ex- pressed regret at having to part with such an excellent member of the staff. He hoped that Mr. Freeman would be as successful in his new position as he had been at Bewsey Chambers. Mr. Freeman suitably replied.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
British and Foreign Bible Society.

The annual meeting of the Warrington aux- iliary of the British and Foreign, Bible Society will be. held on Tuesday evening in the Patten Hall, when a united choir will lead the singing. The Rev. Arthur Bestall, who will be one of the principal speakers, ,is now in England, after 22 years' strennons service. in Biurrnah, where he has .been a pioneer missionary. He served as c'haplain to :the- Forces through the Burmese War, securing the medal and clasp. Realising the sufferings and needs of the lepers, he opened a home for them, an action which awakened the sympathy of all daises and creeds.. He has contributed to the Burmese Sacred Literature by translating the New Testament' in their ow n language. "

===== 0002601_19130920_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
St. John's New Members.

On the occasion of the Communion at St. John's Church, when eighteen new members were received into the Church, the Rev. W. Reid, preaching from the words, "If it be possible, let this cup pass away from Me," spoke of the pos- sible ingredients, of the cup which the Lord was called upon to drain. He referred to the shrinking from death natural to all humanity, to the betrayal by one of the inner circle, and to a world's ingratitude for all that had been done for it. More bitter, however, were the ingredients of a world's wounds and sin arid death, and, bitterest of all, the obscurity which temporarily hid the face of His Father. In the Post-Communion address Mr. Reid referred to the cup which had taken its place, in which peace was substituted for storm.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
The Warrington Leather Trade.

Some yards report, says "The Leather World" -about the Warrington tanning trade, the demand for leather as steady and without much change, but the greater number a more urgent inquiry and a much quickened interest; indeed, the only grumble I met with anywhere was not about the state of trade, but about the little profit there is in, it just now. Manufacturers seem to be in the market for winter weights, as there has been a considerable impetus to, the inquiry for medium to heavy lines, and as sampling and buying are separated by a very small interval now, a fair amount of such goods have already gone out. Middlemen have been operating rather more freely, and the business done must have been considerable. All this tends to keep the position yery hard, and it cannot be a matter, fqr,surprise that in'all three tions one finds prices advancing, and so quickly do these changes come about that on some lines I hear an advance of a half-penny has already been n)ade on quotations current as recently as Manchester Fair.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
trosfleld's Ambulance Division.

The members of the Crosfield Division St. John Ambulance Brigade,. Royal Naval Auxiliary Sick Berth Reserve, assembled for their annual in- spection on Wednesday, under the command of First Officer Cawley. The Inspector was Dr. Stewart; inspecting officer' of the Reserve. The tests included treatment of a man apparently ' drowned and a man found unconscious, the Reservist not 'knowing what had happened to him. The Brigade acted splendidly, and after- wards the Inspector said he was pleased with what he had semi. He also. said there were now' .980-men in the Reserve, and they hoped to reach 1,000 in a week or two. In regard to the annual training, he hoped that next year they would have over 500 members down. He hoped that division would be augmented, and that all the men would take an interest in the work. Ten or a dozen men who were interested were far better than 24 who were uninterested. Captain Wiring and Lieutenant Seymour Jones watched the inspection.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
Meritorious Service Medal.

Mr. J. .11. ,Day, 114, :Padgate;lane, -formerly sergeant-major in the -Ist and 4th Battalions the King's (Liverpool) Regiment, was at the Church Parade, at Orford Barracks,- last .Sunday morn- ing, presented with a medal granted by His Majesty the King as a reward for meritorious service in the Army. Mr. Day enlisted in the King's, March 11th, 1869, and proceeded that year to India. Devotion to duty was rewarded by rapid promotion, for 1872 saw Private Day become a corporal, and 1873 witnessed the cor- poral's advance to the rank of sergeant and colour-sergeant. On the 13th of March, 1878, when he had only nine years and two days ser- vice, he was promoted sergeant-major in the Ist Battalion Bth Foot. Holding this rank for twenty years, he was, for a long time, senior sergeant-niajor in the British Army. After ten zetits' service in India—that is, twelve months after becoming sergeant-major—Mr. Day went to Aden, from which place he was transferred to the Depot in 1883. Captain S. W. Beeman, in making the presen- tation, eulogised the services of Mr. Day, and congratulated him on behalf of the Command- ing Officer and the officers of the regiment. The medal carries with it, besides the distinct honour attached to so rare a reward, tin addi- tional .210 a year to the recipient's pension. Mr. Day's many friends in and out .of the Army will be pleased to know that he has been selected for this favour, for all ranks of his regiment,recog= nised in him a true soldier, while his civilian

friends have always held him in high esteem. PEVERIL FIRE GRATES never fail to please.— Sole agent, J. S. GARLAND, Ironmonger, 51 to 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

A CLASSICAL WEEK.

It says much for the healthy state of the public mind, notwithstandint the many slanders that are spoken of its degeneracy in these modern, rushing days, that a really first- class company with a repertoire of classical plays can attract, to the theatre such large and appreciative audiences as those that have this week welcomed Mr. Alexander Marsh, Miss Carrie Baillie and their talented colleagues on their third annual visit to Warrington. The programme has been 'replete with good things, and the full houses that have been delighted each night have assisted to testify to a condition of all-round satisfaction. "The Three Musketeers," "Oliver Twist," "A Tale of Two Cities," "Nell Gwynne," "The Taming of the Shrew," "David Garrick," and "The Sleigh Bells," offer fare of the choicest kind, and in the hands of this company the fare , has been presented in its most acceptable form: Mr. Marsh as D'Artagnan in "The Three Musketeers" scored a distinct success and went from triumph to triumph as the week pro- ceeded, the thrilling and pathetic part of Syd- ney Carton, last night, being perhaps most suited to his peculiar histrionic powers. Char- acters standing so far apart as Fagin and King Charles 11. came to him with equal ease, and round him the large company played and adapted themselves to their varied and varying fortunes with unvarying success. Of Miss Carrie 13afilie all pie praise it is possible .to give must oi her way. he makes a perfect Lady de Winter, the spy, in The Three Musketeers," Just as she ',gives the exact meaning to Dickens's creation, Nancy, forced to live in the repulsive atmosphere engendered by a Fagin and a Sikes. Nell Gwynne and Lucie Manette also gave full scope for her grace and ability, and when honours were being bestowed Miss Baillie came in for her full share. The Other artistes were uniformly good. Mr. Wilson Gunning as Cardinal Richelieu, Jeffries, Charles Darney and Bill Sikes, and other equally try- ing characters, bore the strain with marked success,his presentation of the burglar-murderer being remarkably fine. Mr. Edwin Beverley stood out prominently as Bumble amid much work of exceptional merit, some of the exag- gerations being excrescences for which he was not responsible. It would be tedious to follow each member of the company through his or her' work of the Week, as it would mean a repetition of praises that were well-earned and richly deserved. To-night "David Garrick" will be presented. and theatre-goers may rely upon the celebrated play being given in such a way as to form a fitting climax to a week's achievements that have been worthy of the reputation of Mr. Marsh's company.

"CLAUDIAN" NEXT WEEk.

Next week Mr. and Mrs. Wm. Maclaren's company are to pay a welcome return visit, after an absence of over two years, with the

late Wilson Barrett's great Roman drama, " Claudian." Mr. Maclaren will take the role of Claudian, the accurseu, which part tie has played 1,695 times. ,fle plays the part with the ease, grace.,anu ueterniniittion, 50, ciatructenauc of the late author-actor. Those who missed the ' opportunity of seeing " Clauclian " on its last visit should not fail to see it this time. \C have been informed that just recently Mt. Maclaren has had all the scenery for the pro-, duction newly paiuted, and new dresses . have been made at great cost. Miss Lily C. Band- mann will play the trying part of Alrnida, and her past success in this and otherplays is guar- antee enough that it could not be placed in better and more capable hands. The great earthquake scene, in whidi Claudian's palace falls in ruins, is know to be the_greatest scene of its kind. Mr. Maclaren and Miss Bandmann will be supported by a company of 50 first-class artistes. Music for the week: Overture, " Claudian," Jones; waltz, "Roses from the South," Strauss; suite, "Merchant of Venice,""RoSse ; Pilgrim's Chorus frdm " Tannhauser " (by request), Wag- ner; Hungarian march, 'Torok- Magyar," Szabadi.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

• The athletic patrons of the HippOdroine have been yell catered for this week, for there are no fewer than three acrobatic and ball punch- ing turns. The remainder of the programme is very entertaining, and seldom have we seen such a brilliant pair of coruedians than Alex- andre and Hughes. They present a refined and smart. comedy act. The Croisit Trio of French acrobats are expert hand balancers and strong

men.' With the greatest of ease they perform the most daring tricks, and one of the troupe gives an exhibition of somersaulting. Selwyn and Willow give a clever ball punching exhibi- tion. Dora Martini appears in a graceful. gym- nastic act. Tile Bros. Leo are a very eccentric

couple. L. \V. Gallagher sings clevei humorous songs. The Six White Boys give a novel vocal and (lancing act, and are successful in stimulat- ing interest in a " line " which the people appdar to have tired of. "He was so fond of animals " is a laughable picture on the cinema.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
Photograph by Birtles, THE LATE WM. BEAMONT.

It issa happy idea of the Warrington Society to perpetuate the memory of the late Mr. Wil- liam Beamont, the ,first Mayor of Warrington, a noted historian and antiquarian, and a man , held, during his 'lifetime, in the highest esteem, by the erection of a memorial tablet on the wall of the office where he dispensed sound legal opinion for half a century. The house, which dates back to 1682, stands conspicuously in Market Place, and the tablet is a worthy com- panion to the other architectural features of the structure. The tablet is the design of Miss Margaret Ellen Clare, of the Warrington School of Art, and other pOrtions of the work . were executed by Mr. Walter A. Clay and Rich- mond's, Ltd. " _

The unveiling ceremon,y was performed yes- terday afternoon by Mr. J. Paul Ryland's, in the presence of a representative gathering,And after that ceremony the' members of the society lunched, at the Old -Academy. . .

The _tablet bore the following inscription

1797-1889, William Beamont,

Historian, Antiquary, and First Mayor of Warrington. Practised here as a Solicitor for 50 years. This tablet was erect- ed in 1913 by The Warrington Society.

Mr. H. STUART PAGE, the chairman of the com- mittee, in asking Mr. Bylands to unveil the tablet, said it was erected to "recognise local patriotism, and they never had a better local 'patriot than William Beamont.. A local patriot was ,a good citizen., and„local patriotism was„as essential to •local progress as national patriot- ism was to national progress. He was not only the first Mayor, but he did much to forward the welfare of the town, and devoted his leisure in recording, its past history. As he said in 'one of his book "A study of the past taught the present how to improve the future."

friend. I remember,. said Mr. Bylands, calling his attention at the Museum to a picture and other things that wanted renovating. He said, get the things done, and when they were finished I gave him the bills. The next. time I saw Bea- mont was at the committee meeting, and I asked him what about the bills. "Don't say anything about them," answered -Beamont, "I have paid them and the committee need never know anything about them." (Laughter and applause.) That was the, way Mr. Beamont had of doing things. In the 'saiiie way he gave much _money away in secret to help institutiOns and other things that had his sympathy. Stilt he was a man who could be parsimonious. I remember somebody Saying about him thnt "he was close- fisted, very closerfisted." I remember him showing, me a rare book, the back of which he had bound with brown papeminstead of taking it, to a bookbinder. I said: "It is a pity to bind ,such a book with brown paper," .and his tinsWe'r was : " What does it matter ; it anSwers the purpose. It is quite strong enough." (Laughter.) He bound it wilt' brown paper rather than spend a shilling upon it et the book- binder's, yet he would perhaps. give £2O away to 90me charity the next minufe. That reminded, him, added Mr. Bylands, of the nablOniiii-*-ho; gave a crergyman 4100 to get a holiday tolinprove his health, at the same time returning the clergyman's- visiting. cards, which had been left during calls at .the hall, with the remark that he had saved them, as it was a pity to waste the cards, which could be used again. (Laughter.) ,

===== 0002601_19130920_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
AS A PERSONAL FRIEND.

I take it as a great compliment, Said Mr. J. PAUL BYLANDS, prior to unveiling the tablet, to be asked to unveil this to the memory of my old and personal friend. He was the 'greatest anti- quarian we ever* had in Warrington, and his work is a work that will live, He Was a man of mature and sound judgment and of indom- itable industry. I um perfectly. certain William Beamont helped Warrington very much at the time when they were beginning a new form of municipal government. , He was also one Of ,the most charitable of Men we ,ever. had in :the. loWit. Educational .work always. appealed Strongly tro -him and he' was the .founder of Heath-street Council Sehools,- while he 'also- founded- the Museum, Of Whieh' he was Sol proud. - Mr. Rylands then unveiled the tablet, and afterwards- the company assembled in tkie Old Academy for lunch.. It was often a wonder, said Mr. Bylands, how Beamont got through the immense amount of historical work he did., IL was the result of Very wide reading, and I have seen him jot down on odd pieces of paper any points from books about Warrington to be incorporated ,in his works later. His work took him through ,a very wicke circle of reading; because he could not get the information, even in the -British Museum, through indexed public records as we can now.

William Beamont has been severely criticised both in his life-time and after his death over grain inaccuracies- and shortcomings, but I think we ought to remember that he was a pioneer, and it was impossible for a man to do the work in Beamont's days with the same accuracy as it can now be'idone. ===== 0002601_19130920_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RESOURCES AND ADVANTAGES OF WARRINGTON . AS, A ' MANUFACTURING CENTRE.

'Very few towns are so well adapted as War- rington is for manufacturing purposes: Its geographical position is excellent. It is situated near to the coalfields,. and, being within 18 miles of Liverpool, one of the leading ports of the world, enables it to receive its ratv materials and dispatch its manufactures with greater facility than most towns. The Ship Canal, too, running along its boundary, is of considerable advantage—as War- rington, at the time the Ship Canal Co. applied for powers to make the Canal, supported the scheme—it received a special advantage in respect of goods forwarded by the Canal.. Hallway communication is also above the average, there being at least three branches passing through the town. The town, owning and possessing an electri- cal undertaking, giveS a further advantage to manufacturing of certain classes, as by the supply of electric motor power from one central source saves some manufacturers of being otherwise compelled to build furnaces and other plant. Thus a saving of capital is effected. The town, too, being on a level or flat sur- face, makes it easy for cartage and haulage purposes. It has, for example, been stated that

===== 0002601_19130920_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
one horse in Warrington can effect as much work as two horses, say, in towns like Oldham 'or Stockport, which are hilly .and difficult. Warrington, too, is possessed of an excep- tionally good and abundant water supply—both for manufacturing and domestic purposes.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
LANDLORDS MORE EQUITABLE- THAN FORMERLY.

I think that it will be found that landowners will be more equitable and judicious in their terms for land required than was formerly the ease; as It is not likely the mistake made with reference to Lever and his negotiations for Inn:1 in Warringkin some years ago will be repeated. It might be wise, as has been suggested before, if the Warrington Corporation made a point of adVertislhg 'the advantages • and re- sources of Warrington, and•probably at the same time be good policy, as has occurred in some other towns, of offering to new manu- facturers special rate terms for the first five years, subject, of course, to the new manufaC- tures being of quite a different nature to those existing at present in the town.

CANAL v. THE RIVER.

There was a time when the Warrington Cor- poration seriously thought of having the River Mersey developed for the benefit of: the town. This was before the Ship Canal was niade. At that time he late Alderman Joseph Davies. a former mayor, was the leading spirit in the town. The Corporation went sti . far. As ..to. engage Mr. Wolfe 'Barry, who, was considered to be the then leading Britiskengineer, to give a reporL.! This report, I. believe, is now lodged in some Pigeon hole in the Warrington-Town Hall. Many people at. that time thought it

would have been wisp if Warrington had had sufficient enterprise to have seriously gone into the scheme, that it would have led to huge developments, similar to those of the Clyde at , Glasgow. Rightly or wrongly, other counsels Prevailed,,and whether;now,....as the ship Canal is made, finally puts an end to such a scheme. personally I do not feel myself competent to iudge. It is, however, interesting to note thitt traffic along the river is on the increase,- and no one can really foretell what the future may bring forth. In any event, taking most of the gifts of Nature into account, very few towns can exeel Warrington for the arts of manufacture. It is n 1 y called the "Town ,of Many Industries.", Personally. I take an optimistic view of the future outlook of Warrington.

E. ENGLAND.

===== 0002601_19130920_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
Certificated Supply P

AND WHAT WILL IT COST?

I have in front of me at this moment a pic- ture in a gilt frame, protected by a pane of glass. It consists of 36 small pieces of white cloth pasted on to a square of cardboard in columns. These are pieces of sterilised cloth, and through•each one of them has been poured a pint of milk, after it reached a certain dairy in London (and before it was dyed pale brown to please the London public). On the first 12 pieces you can just perceive, if you look closely', that the centre, of the piece of cloth has a pasty look, but the paste is as white as the cloth itself. That milk comes from a certain farm where every precaution known is taken to pre- vent contamination.

Preventing Contamination.

The method is as follows: The cow is groomed and washed before she is milked; the milkman's hands are washed, and he wears a clean white overall; the barn is cleaned out thoroughly twice a day; the milking can is an American one, covered right over the top to

prevent, the dirt falling in,the milk running into it through, an aperture in the side; the can is washed out and sterilised before each time of

using it. Immediately after milking, the milk is cooled (to prevent the development of bacteria), poured into sterilised bottles or churns, sealed, and consigned to, the dairy. The herd are tested regularly fo'r tuberculosis with tubercu- lin, and diseased cows removed immediately; the water supply fbr, the cows is looked after to make sure of no, 6ontamination. Tests are taken of the milk every day under the micro- scope, and the number of bacteria per cubic centimetre in each man's milk is posted up on Ithe barn door._ The milkmen take a keen in- terest in this, and there is competition among them to get the lowest count of bacteria. This, can generally be kept down to abo\tt 10,000 per cubic centimetre,.the average number found in ordinary London) milk as supplied to the public being 2,000,000. The milk produced by this farm is up to the standard required by the New York Board' before they will permit the producer to label: his milk "Certified," and anyone in New York' State - using the word " Certed " in labelling' or advertising his milk, without the permissiOrc of the Board, is liable

to prosecution. To return to the pieture; rfe next 12 pieces Vary, some of them being a little grimy. These represent filterings. or the milk of several- farmers. A sample of their milk is filtered and the cloth posted off tb diem at once. When the cloth shows dirt, at marked improvement in the filtering of that farmer's milk will be ob- served next day. These. 24 pieces complete the sampling of the, milk usually sold to the public by this dairy: Now we come to the last 12 pieces. What do they represent? for at first sight they remind one of an illustration in a history of ancient copper coins! They are the filterings of one- pint of milk taken from each

of 12 churns of milk, bought from a well-known West End London dairy' on a day when our dairy had to meet a much greater demand for milk .than usual. The 12 pieces Vary, of course, but every one shows an ample supply of dirt, enough to make it look more like a disc of earth or metal. than a sample of milk. This aPpear- anca is due to dirt, largely cbw-dung, I.vhic4i has not dissolved in the mak: during its journey to, Londbp.. ; A.' few years ago it " was calculated that the people or Chicago imbibed 25 tons of dirt with their milk. 'Needless to say, dirt that makes a filteringof milk look like an old copper coin is not wholesome, but 'still gives us no in- formatiou abouV the bacteria, tuberculous and other, which may or may not be in. the milk.

Raw Milk Best—if nue.

Here is a complete scheme for getting raw milk on to the market as near perfection as is humanly possible. The only weak point in it is this: Is the tuberculin test for bovine phthisis absolutely reliable? If it is, Mr. Burns will be making, us a handsome present of im- munity from disease if he will give legislative protection to the word " Certified " understood in this sense, and with its use controlled by a reliable Board. This is raw milk, and scientific research has some recent news to give us about the importance of raw milk. Recently some young rats were fed upon milk-powder and water, that is, something which chemical analy- sis showed to be absolutely pure and perfect milk, the perfect food. They all developed rickets and died. Another set were fed upon a starvation quantity of ordinary milk, that is not dessicated or "purified." The amount was so small that they might have been expected to die of inanition. They all thrived. The ex- periment demonstrated that there is something In raw milk which chemical analysis cannot discover, but which is essential to life.

Scientific Whisperings That Torment Us. " Certified " 'milk, as described above, costs at present &I. per-quart to put on the market; ordinary, milk costs 4d. -Again, there is so muth tuberculosis in the herds of England, that if all the diseased cows were slaughtered, the farmers, and the local ratepayers as well, if they ' had to 'compensate them, would be ruined; and' tile price of cows and milk Would gd.up greatly. Pending the.- gradual improve-. went of the health of the cows, Baron Pfeiff appears with a simple steam heat process, only requiring a little boiler, easily set up on any ' farm. Raise you milk, he says to 165 degrees Fahrenheit immediately after milking. You won't boil it or sterilise it, for that spoils it, but you'll kill all the bacteria, including tuber- cule. Then bottle and seal it, and you will find it keeps good for six days; this can be marketed for 4d. per quart, the present price. The Baron can't tell us whether that "elixir vitae" which proved SQ valuable to the young rats can sur- vive his 165 degrees Fahrenheit, nor can any- one else, since there is no process of analysis which can detect its presence or its absence. Other scientific • whisperings torment our wearied ear. Some bacteria are very good: you can't afford to lose them; and we are • thrown back upon a thirst for that raw milk we heard of just now. Mr. Burns's army of Inspector's will be useful, if expensive; while his municipal milk depots may ,be expected to serve the public about the ,same generous pro- portion of dirt and disease, that the " beg dairies do to-day. .To whomsoever he entrusts, IL, the 'discovery and slaughter of tuberculous cattle will, if successful, hit individuals and districts very hard, while in the background •we hear another section of scientists assuring us that we cannot take consumption through our stomachs, whatever we put into them. "Who can decide, when doctors disagree?"— even about killing cows?

FINANCES OF THE RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The half-yearly balance sheet of the War. rington Rural District Council is published by Mr. J. C. Sutton, the clerk, and this shows an income of .C 7,790 48. 10d., of which £4,815 4s. 3d. was a balance brought forward, and ,t 1,990 was received from the overseers for general expenses. The balance carried forward to next half-year is £3.753 15s. sd. The work of the Council has thus been admirably performed at the cost of £3,936 9s. sd. for the half-year. The heaviest item of expenditure was £2,409 19s. 6d. for th.e maintenance and repair of roads other than main roads, and doubtless - the . heavy motor traffic is responsible for a good deal of this.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

The EDITOR of the " Exaininer " invites corre- spondence on all subjects of, public interest.

LETTERS should be brief and pointed. Their object must be to impart or seek information, not abuse.

LUTTERE must in all cases be accompanied by the name and address of the writer. COMMUNICAtIONS received early in the week— except urgent matters—can always be given the best attention.

A WARRINGTONIAN IN WESTERN AUSTRALIA.

Sir,--AS many farm labourers in England have a desire to leave their native land to seek their fortune in some British Colony, and., as many advertisements in the English Press are very misleading, I would be glad if you would kindly publish the enclosed letter, which was published in yesterday's issue of the West Australian newspaper. I do not know .the writer, but I can endorsa all that he says. Farni labourers are not wanted near so much as men who can "swing" the axe.

WARRINGTONIAN.

IMMIGRANTS AND FARM worIK.

To the Editor.

Sir,—Seeing that a slur has been cast upon "Pommies" in Parliament, I write to know if the lion. inemper who said it is a "pommy " himself. Where the mistake is made is in London, where the Agent-General's Office says there is a demand for farm hands, when it is the strong "buck-navvy •". who is wanted, who can use a shovel, an axe, and break .wire with his teeth. I am speaking from experience as I have had a good spell of it in different parts of the State 1 lie cocky would be satisfied with a itavivy, and could keep a few pigs and fowls to enable him to do his ploughing and seeding. Farming is a scientific operation, but in West- ern Australia it's a case of sand scratching, wheat sowing and wait for rain. I ,C1111!C here with two farm hands who were 50n...4 of big farmers in England, and they were experienced in all branches, yet when they are, in .the bush they are just ordinary clearer, with a short Spell of ploughing, ticc., I was, a prize plough-, 'man at home; and sent my.eard.to the agent, who said I should have no difficulty in getting 'X2 a. week and keep. • But up to now I can't get more than 30s. a week, and most of the year I am working with ihe axe. If the pamphlets that are circulated at home would speak of con- ditions which exist in the bush, namely, tinned dog, tents .and temperature, I have no doubt "pommies" would stay at horne,-and enjoy life. it only on a living wage, wholesome food, and good housing. Members of Parliament who live in luxury would try to fix a standard wage. If they could have a few months' " holiday " in the bush, their opinions would then be "A feed in the town is worth two in the bush."—Yours,

DEVONSIIIRE DUMPLING.

Perth, August 6.

LESSONS OF DUBLIN. "A Reply to Phil &Ind."

Sir,--No one believed English Tory human nature capable of resisting the temptation to utilise the unfortunate disturbances in Dublin for the political ends dear to. the hearts of Sir Edward Carson, the Duke of Norfolk, Lord Edmund Talbot, Mr. Bonar Law:---and, perhaps, Phil Squod." However, one Unionist. Paper admits that no political significance could be attached to the unhappy conflicts in Dublin." But our friend, "Phil Sctuod," appears to have 'followed the majority, and taken up the gospel of the "Morning Post" and "Globe," as in- augurated by an excellent Irish politician, Mr. Louis Garvin. The Dublin events are certainly deplorable, but, leaving aside the question of right or wrong, as connected with the claims advanced by the working classes Itiere, it should not be forgotten by " Phil Squod " that the recent proceedings in the Irish capital have been conducted on English principles, controlled by ar, individual:- trained , in Laneitshire, And familiar With pr iriciPles and methods of "InClUS- triztl:w3Trara,"ld and practised On this side of the Irish Sea., rather than On the Other. Has Squoit " forgotten. the strikes which• have taken place in England within the last four years? But even if he has he cannot have for-- gotten the melees" in Derry and Belfast. Surely these are far moire depraved, far more despicable, than have been the affairs of Dub- lin. The Tory party in an endeaVour to find a policy, turn to Ireland for some "scare," and the latest is that "a foretaste a Horne.Bule.has been witnessal."

' Well, I am glad that' "Phil Squod ", admits that we shall have a native Parliament, and I might inform him that the degree of strength which an Irish Government will bring to bear upon diffel.ences between employers and ern- ployes will not be regulated by the extent of the armed forces at their disposal, but upon the Government's capacity to Provide the Machinery for a lair adjustment of rights and claims. An Irish administration animated by no other Motive than a desire and determination to advance National Interests by securing fair play for all classes and between all classes, will find comparatively little difficulty in solving he problems that have been created in Ireland mainly by British inis-goVernment, and that Eritish administrators can never effe(:tively deal with. This cheap insult regarding Dublin is 16st-id on,ruisleading..preMises, and still more false is.. Che impudent assumption • that Irish nationality is identified 1 with the passing phases of a local struggle in an Irish city. In corrlusion,.l would .remind "Phil Squod " that the very section of the population—the poticP—Who caused the disturbarres, or I should say, the Melee, have been the curse of the Irish people for years. Does " Phil Squod- hide his . face from the evictions which have taken place in Ireland, and, ignoring the work of the police, landlord, military and battering rare, dwell ,upon Pharisaical dissertations on kliAirYing spectacles in Did

F. W. n

HOME RULE NOT A RELIGIOUS QUESTION.

have froni time to 11me read Many l:Aters on various subjects from your corre- sPondent, "Phil Squod,7 agreed with pinny, sympathised with some', and . difiered with others, hut, have always appreciated the earnest spirit of conviction in all. His latest effort, in your columhs of the 13th, has,' however, occa- sioned me some surprise: Dews "Phil Squod " really think the Home Buie Lin introduced by the present Government is on experimental ruetisurp, - brought in on pp! of the illorneni?' Or MS' he forgotten It is the fulfil- ment of a Pledge and the outconie (.3f the de- clared policy of the Liberal party for the past 1p years, viz., to attempt to settle the Irish dint-

culty in accordance with the declared wishes of the majority* of the 'lrish people, whilst-main- taining the supremacy of the Imperial Parlia- ment?

Does "Phil Squod " believe that Home Rule will really promote and advance Boman Catholicism in Ireland? .If so, will he kindly explain or reconcile the opposition to it of the Duke of 'Norfolk; •Marquis of Bute, Earl, of Den- bigh; Lords Lovat,- Petrei'' and the leading Catholics of England? Canvass your leading English Catholics in Warrington, and find how very few are supporters of Home ,Rule, and yet it, is not because they ti re indifferent to the interests of their Church. Their sacrifices for it in the paqt, and their efforts for it in the present, sufficiently contradicts that view. Irish Nationalists remember with veneration and . pride the Irish Protestants, such as Lord Edward Fitzgerald, Robert Emmett, Wolfe Tone, John Mitchel, John Martin, Smith O'Brien, Isaac. Butt, Shaw, Parnell and hun- dreds of others, who, in the past, have given, sonic their' lives, many their liberty, and count- less others suffered exile and social ruin, all for ,their love of Ireland and Iheir efforts against its! misgovernment. Does "Phil Squod" think they suffered this to promote the interests of Boman Catholicism? Does he think the Pro- testants amongst the Irish Nationalists are also animated by the same desire? Not at. All. Irish 'Nationalism rises above all sectarianism. Its aim is religious equality for all; privilege or ascendancy for none.

Why "Phil Squod " introduced the ,present strike trouble In Dublin as an argument against Home Rule, I don't quite understand, as it seems to me rather an argument on the other side. Does he assume the recent strikes in Bel- fast and Londonderry are an endorsement of the spirit of content with things as they are? I want. to say here, what I have °Mimes said in -other place: The man who opposes •Home Rule because of a patriotic belief that it is in- imical to the best interests; politically :and economically, of the empire, I can respect and honour his views. It is our•business,to try and convince him otherwise. .But my reading of the history of the past, and my experience of the present, teaches me that religious bigotry and intolerance is not peculiar- to oneparAicular

"The trail of the serpent is over them all.",

The Pigeon Keepers.—There was another dis- cussion at the meeting of the Parochial Com- mittee on Tuesday, over the keeping of pigeons in some parts of the district. —Councillor West- brook said he believed notice had been given to persons in Heath-street to desist from keeping pigeons. He wanted to know if they had com- plied with the notice.—The Clerk said some had, but they might have to take action to have the request carried Out. —Councillor Whitley : It is not infrequent to count 20 or 30 pigeons on the roof of one house. The slates are white with deposits from the birds, and in wet weather it gets washed into the spouts and tanks.—Coun- cil-lor Westbrook: It is a menace to health.— Councillor Whitley': The stench from the water in which there is pigeon deposit is abominable. —The Sanitary Committee were left to deal fur- ther with tho matter.

Let There be Light.—The Parochial Com- mittee had before them on Tuesday the tender from Warrington Gas Department for the fix- ing of two lamps at the dangerous corner at Lumb Brook. The Corporation of Warrington offered to extend the main and fix the lamps at an extra charge of ss. per lamp per annum.— Councillor Westbrook: Extra to what?—The Clerk: To the usual charge. It is in respect of the extra length of main.—Councillor McDer- mid : That will be a continuous chargeF—The Chairman: Yes. I have no fear that the Latch- ford Without people will also have a lamp put up.—Councillor Whitley:. I think it is an ex- cessive charge. —Councillor Westbrook: It is only a charge so long as there_ arf? no gas colt- Burners along the lane. We should not hesitate to light this danger spot for the sake of ss. The Chairman Chairman (Councillor Pilling) : I think it a reasonable charge. The last time Warrington wanted double that price.—Finally it was agreed to accept the condition.

An Interesting Anniversary.—lnteresting an- niversary services were held at the independent Methodist Church, Stockton Heath, on Sunday. The preacher in the morning, and again in the evening, was Councillor Robert Henshall, of Latchford. He preached two impressive dis- courses. A special service for the young people was held in the afternoon, and this, like the other two gatherings, was well attended. An inspiring address was given by Mrs. Moore, of Warrington. The choir ably rendered special anthems, under the conductorship of Mr. E. E. Roberts. Mr. James Bellamy ably played the accompaniments on the organ. The collections amounted to over 025 10s, A Profitable Bible Class.—The winter session of the Bible Class, which meets at the School-- room' of the Independent Methodist Church on Wednesday evening, is likely to prove the most profitable and interesting on record. The class leader, Mr. A. Mounfield, is to give a series of addresses on "How Christianity was planted in the Roman Empire." The course is expected to last/the whole winter, and as Mr. Mounfield is an expert upon such subjects the addresses ought to prove educative, as well as attractive. The Revision Court.—The Revision Court for the Stockton Heath side of the linutSford Divi- sion was held at Stockton Heath on Wednesday. Th,e Revising Barrister was Mr. C. E. Lloyd, and helhad no problems to deal with. He went over the lists presented by the following Assistant Overseers: Mr. E. Bradshaw, Stockton Heath; Mr. J. Ellison, Apnleton and Stretton ; Mr. J. Smith, Latchford Without; Mr. H. Lawrence. Walton Inferior and part of Warrington; and Mr. Molesdale, Walton Superior. The Revising Barrister congratulated Mr. Bradshaw upon the excellent way in which the list had been pre- pared. There were no claims of special interest.

PRECAUTIONS AGAINST FIRE. A Telephone to the Brewery.

Stockton Heath is now to be doubly -guarded against fire; The local brigade are to be slightly better equipped and • are tt• be encouraged to practice more, while arrangements have now been.made to get the services of the Wilderspool 'Brewery Brigade. *Councillor McDermid, -at the monthly meet- ing of the Parochial 'Committee on Tuesday, .re, ported upon the arrangements., ,He said repre- sentatives of the committee had waited upon Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. with refer- ence to the question or telephonic communica- tion for the purpose of giving alarms of fire. It was originally suggested that the telephonic installation should Ibe extended from the general office of the firm to the lodge, in order that the brigade could easily be summoned in the night. The firm, however, put forward the alternative that direct communication should be established with the house of Mr. Croughton, the captain of the brigade, Wilderspool-cause- way. The cost would be £7 ss. .

It was further proposed that Mr. Croughton should, by pressing a button, be able to sum- mon the other members of the brigade at their own homes, as could already be done by electric communication with the lodge at the brewery. The Fire Appliances Committee recommended that the suggestion of the firm be adopted, and that the Parochial Committee pay one-half the cost of the telephonic extension. Councillor MoDermid added that when the arrangements had been carried out, they would stand in a vary favourable position. They had at disposal a well-equipped brigade, and in one tespect it was perhaps better than almost. any other local brigade, -because the men were all resident on the spot, and they worked at the same place during the day. That was an advan- tage over the Warrington brigade, which had to gather the men from all quarters when the alarm was given.

"Weakness of the Scheme."

Councillor Westbrook said the weakness of the scheme seemed to be that they were de- pendent, at one point, • upon someone being in Mr. Croughton's house. Could there be a guar- antee that, there would always be someone in attendance, as in the case of the lodge at Wilderspool? Councillor MeDermid said Mr. Croughton had informed them that his house was occupied from one year's end to the other. The committee had accepted -the tender 6f Messrs. Merryweather for the supply of certaiti additional articles of equipment at *a cost of £l6 17s. Cd., for the Stockton Heath brigade. When those had' been obtained the committee Considered that their Present arrangements would he sufficient to meet all ordinary cases of fire—in roc*. when the brigade was thoroughly organised they would be able to deal with most fires likely to occur in the district', without outside assist- ance.

At the present time, he said, there was some diffidence on the part of the men in turning out for practices, owing to the ridicule to which the local brigade had been subjected, while the inducements which the committee had to offer were not very strong. It had beeh decided to arrange a meeting between the committee and prospective firemen to go fully into the matter before calling another practice. He believed the outcome would be the securing of a very good brigade, suitable for all their purposes. With the services of the Wilderspool brigade at their disposal, they would not require any more elaborate precautions. Councillor McDermid moved and Councillor Mclntyre seconded, the report, which was adopted.

To Save a Long Walk.—At a meeting of the Stockton Heath Parish Council on Tuesday— Councillor Whitley • presiding Councillor Mclntyre suggested that a TOOM should be se- cured in Stockton Heath to save people who wished to see the parish doctor having to walk to Stretton. This is due to the strike at the Man- chester Docks. Not ,one-fourth, of the usual number of -vessels have passed through this week. The sirens, which usually ,disturb tne. silence of the ; night • have been qui4., and it is 'reported that some people along the canal hanks cannot sloop at nights because of the absence of the hoarse " music" from the sirens. Verily, the dock strikers have much to answer for! Death of Mrs. Swift.—Tbe interment took place on Saturday of Mrs. Swift, wife of Mr. John Swift, of Grappenhall-road. The deceased lady passed away at the age of 73, and was much respected and beloved. She leaves a husband, a sort and daughter, by whom her loss will be keenly felt. The funeral took place at the Hill Cliff° Graveyard, and was conducted by the Rev. G. Curtis. The chief mourners were Mr John Swift (husband), Mr. Swift (son), Mr. Bowden, of Frodsham (nephew), and Mr. C. Plinston. - -

Death of Mr. Thomas Metcalfe.—The death hai occurred of Mr. Thomas Metcalfe, of Mar- bury-street, in his 50th year. The deceased gentleman was a native of Yorkshire, but came to Warrington thirteen years ago. He has been identified with the Primitive Methodist Church in Knutsford-road, but owing to employment out of town has been less closely ateclied to the church in recent years. For a long period he *as a, preacher upon the Primitive Metho- dist plan. He was of studious habit and a lover of books, and has filled a useful place in the church to which be belonged. The funeral was conducted by the Rev. Mr. Chessum. Whence the Odours?—At the meeting of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday, the ,Clerk re- ported that in answer to the letter ordered to be sent to the tenant of Cobb's Farm about the alleged stench, the farmer now wrote that the cause of the trouble was being removed.—Coun-; cill,or Whitley said he visited the place. More Houses Wanted.—Everybody seems to want to reside in Stockton Heath, and the de- mand for houses is reported to exceed the supply. Over 59 new houses have been erected. during the past few months, and they were Often oceu- pied before quite finished. We have had names down for the houses," said. a builder, "before the foundations were cut, and still the cry is for more. Newly-married people, or those about to be married, are chiefly the tenants. One wedding, I am told, was put off for six weeks in order that the couple might wait for a new house being ready. Stockton Heath.. if the, building continues, will soon be easily the most populous district under the Rural District Council." Miss Capell sang the. sole with much .taste and ,expression. Thb Rev. Mr. Llewellyn preached another interest- ing sermon in the evening, and the festival prayed a great success. The collections pro- duced over £9.

THE BOYS AND THE STONES. Is a Recreation Ground Required?

Boys of Stockton Heath are reported ,to be emtilating King David, in that they are* expert stone throwers. The Goliaths .they aim at are street sign-boards, and it was reported' at a meeting of the Parochial= Committee on 'Tues- day—Councillor Pilling presiding—that consid- erable damage had been done. Mr. J. Pemberton, Alexandra-road, wrote to - the committee drawing attention to wanton damage do'ne to enamelled signs at street corners in that neighbourhood. In Pike-street and Hawthorn-grove the wording was almost obliterated, while the first-named sign was drop- ping from the wall. The dardage was done by youths throwing stones and chipping the enamel and he contended the appearance of the pro- perty was depreciated. He suggested that the •nuisance might be stopped by posting notices threatening prosecution. Mr. Pemberton added that Gaskell-street and adjoining thoroughfares were made to look untidy because paper, was carelessly thrown about by householders and children.

Difficulty _to Catch Offenders.

Mr. Swinton, the Siiroi7,...s—ald—the com- plaints were well founded. The difficulty was .catch the transgressors and obtain witnesses to support a prosecution. 'Hawthorn-grove was shortly to be made under the Private Streets Works Act,- and there would 'then be little chance of children finding loose stones to throw. He suggested that if quiet streets were tar - sprayed it would be an economical course to adopt, as it would not only fasten the macadam and make it impossible for children to root it up. but less street watering would be required. Councillor- Westbrook said stone-throwing at street name-plates was :a matter ,to which the attention of the police should be called: It would be a enrious thing if they had to go. to the expense of tar-spraying to prevent children

LINES FROM LYMM.

Happy are the motorists!, For they shall have two suits of clothes. An automobilist wa.g.stopped by the police. He was asked to present his licence. The mOtorist'had'not got it. At the Petty Sessions two Suits of clothes were sAid tb-be the cause.

It was explained that he had changed from one suit'intd-another.' • And in the transfer he had forgotten " unfor- tunately " to transfer .the licence. Why should it be "unfortunate"? Many folk would feel fortunate If they had more than one suit. That recalls another Lymin story. An Irish farm labourer Visited the township. He stood admiring a fine travelling box at a shop door. The shopkeeper, with an eye to,busness, said, "You should buy it,.Pat."

"And, shure, Mr. -, it's meself that's axing phat Oi'd do wid it?" "Put your clothes and boots in it, Pat," answered the shopkeeper. "Put me boots and clothes in it? Shure, Mr. -, yez widn't have me go naked?"

Pat's arguthent unanswerable, .arid nc) trade was done in that direction.

" Siow throw them to me," said the angler The dealer asked why.

"There is it geubby element about the.matter; after all." said a Lymm wag.. ," The Revision Court is held to comply with the provisions Of an 'Act of Parliament."

Behind the Cross.--For having used ob-seene language, Alfred Brazendale, 'tower Cottage, I4ymm, was charged at the Lymm Petty Sessions this week.—lt was stated that defendant was struggling with two men and using very bad language. Afterwards he walked behind the Cross and used bad language again.

, The Petty, Sessions.—Mr. A. W. Callander pre- sided at the Lymm Sessions on Wednesday, and Mr. S. Davies and Mr. John Coxon also adjudi- cated. There were a number of eases down for hearing, but the Most interesting was the assault case arising out of dispute of tenancy of a local garden.

Unfortunately?—Thomas Clare, 272, Knuts- ford-road, Warrington, was, at the Lymzn Petty Sessions on Wednesday, lined 2s. 6d. for failing to produce his motor licence when requested by Sergeant Washington.—Defendant was driving a motor cycle and side-car, and when taxed by the officer he said he had left it at home. He had since forwarded it to the police.—Defendant said that " unfortunately " he had More than one suit of clothes, and by an oversight he had loft the licence in another pocket.

A Dentist's Licence.—Ernest C. Barlow, dental surgeon, 54, Shaw-road, Oldham, was summoned, at the Lyiam Sessions on Wednesday, for failing to produce a motor licence when requested to'do so by Sergeant Washington.—Mr; J. A. K. Ferns (Stockport) represented defendant. —Sergeant Washington. said Barlow was driving a motor cycle and he put his hand up. Defendant stopped, and he (the sergeant) said: "I think you are driving too. fast round this !corner." 'Asked for his licence, def,'?nelant showed one that had expired on August 11th, which was a week previous to the day when he was caught.-7-Mr. Ferns submited tliat, he had no case to answer. The licence was produced and the name and address were correct. —After a legal argument, the magistrates ruled that a licence which had run Out was not a licence in the sight Of the

law. They ordered defendant to pay 55., includ- ing costs.

-Found Dead in a Field.—Mr. H. C. Yates; county coroner, held an inquest on the body of john Holroyd Robinson, at Cross Farm, High Legh, on Wednesday aftsrnoon. It appears that the deceased, a man of 71 years of ago, was an-employe of Mr. John -Henry Wright, of Cross Farm, High Legh, and on Tuesday was engaged in gathering potatoes. Robinson was subject to fits, and it is supposed. that whilst he was work- ing he had a seizure and fell face downwards between two drills, with the result that ho was suffocated. The verdict was "Death from suffo- cation whilst in an apoplectic fit."

"A Tissue of Lies."—At the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Angeld and Albert Di Lorenzo, 24, Dial-street, Warrington, were sum- moned for having assaulted Thomas Jennings, 48, School-brow, Warrington, at Thelwall on the 11th inst.—Complainant did not appear, and Mr. Jenkins, who zybresented the defendants, said he had not expected complainant to appear.— The alleged complaint was that defendants kicked Jennings and "punched him like a dog," and hit him on the head and side, and Jennings had had to go to the Warrington Infirmary.— Mr. Jenkins said it was a disgraceful proceeding for the complainant to take. Ile suffered from bad kip and his statement that the defendants kicked his legs was a tissue of lies. There had been some bother between Jennings' employer and the Di Lorenzos' parents. It was simply a revengeful act on the part of Jennings. There was no assault at all, Jennings and young ChiceOli passed the defendants at the Pickering Arms and there was no bother. It was a wicked proceeding, and he asked for all costs and the advocate's fee in both cases.—The case was dis- missed, and the advocate's fee for ..£1 is., with all other costs, was all Owed. Tale of the Gas.—A curious ease was heard at the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday, when Mrs. Charlotte Highfield, Moston-street, New- field, was summoned by the Council for the pay- ment of 9s. owing for gas consumed, and for damage, amounting to 35., to the slot meter.— Mr. Donaldson said that 2,700 feet of gas had been consumed. There should have been 9s. 4d. in the meter, but when he went to the house there was nothing in the box, but Mrs. Highfield gave him fourpence, which, she said, she hact found on the floor. The tinwork of the meter box was wrenched and broken, and the lock was damaged. A poker was, found_ near by.—Defend- ant said that she knew nothing about the damage. She left the key outside when she Went out one day. On the Sunday morning her (laughter went to put a penny in the meter and it dropped' out of the box. They, informed the police that it had been broken open: That was ' the. second time it had been broken open.—Mr. Mnllard : We have had Several of these eases in Lymm.—Tha case was adjourned for a month, in order that defendant might pay 16s. 6d.
