DISPUTE OVER A GARDEN. An Interesting Lymm Case.

The disputed tenancy of a garden behind a local wheelwright' shop had a sequel at the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday, when Horace Clarke was summoned by Hannah Clarke, Heatley Heath, Heatley, for assault. Mr. Keogh, who appeared for the complainant, said Mrs. Clarke was a widow and was 68 years of age. Defendant was a blacksmith and had tenanted the next house to Mrs. Clarke. Behind defendant's wheelwright's shop there was a garden, which belonged to complainant. Defend- ant had ,been trying to get possession of the garden, which was the reason for the assault. Whenever the old lady went into the garden defendant had tried to prevent her, and for the last two years the old lady's life had been miser- able. -

On one occasion she was taking up some weeds, and defendant, who was not then the tenant of the smithy, told 'her she had no right there. •Ile took the weeds and threw them in her face, causing her to fall backwards on some iron and springs. She bruised her side, shoulder and leg, and was attended by a doctor. . Mrs. Clarke said defendant had not paid any rent for the garden for the past two years. She had been told by the owner that she could have it. Defendant's time was up for the garden and the smithy in June. Defendant: If I had to be ejected, why are my things not removed? I have got a receipt (pro- duced) which shows that the garden is mine yet. I own the garden until March 25th and the smithy till Christmas. In answer to the magistrates, he gave an undertaking not to interfere with the old lady again, and the case was dismissed.

POINTS AT THE REVISION COURT. An Interesting Tory Claim.

Mr: A. Colt Williams attended at the Lymm Police Court on Thursday to revise the voters' list in connection with the Lymm district of the, A ltrincham Division. Messrs. S. Thomson (legal agent), A. W. Callendar, C. E. Ardern and T. Bradbury represented the Conservatives, and Messrs. H. Hetherington and R. Barker ap- peared for the Liberals. file most interesting case was that in which the Conservatives claimed an occupier's vote for James Shepherd in respect of land and tene- monis in Bridgewater-street, Eagle-brow and Pepper-street, Lymm. In answer to Mr. Hetherington, claimant said that the land was in Pepper-street, and Mr. Smith was the landlord. There were two tene- ments, in addition to the land in Pepper-street. He admitted there were separate :landlords. Mr. Hetherington objected to the vote on the ground that claimant could not join ;the land and tenements under separate landlords to make up the requisite value for a sole qualification. Mr. Thomson: It has been done in the Revi- sion Court at Manchester. _

Mr. Hetherington: There has been no test ,:ase since the 1884 Act

After each side had quoted cases, the Revis- ing Barrister said he would look into the par- ticulars of the cases mentioned, and 'give his decision later: When the values were givenit was found that claimant was"entitled to a vote in regard•-th the land alone. _

• Wliams: Why did you want the threc in V Mr. Thomson: We do not need ele three; we have enough value on the land. The vote was granted. The Liberals raised an objection to the lodger vote of Thomae Hattcn, Booths Hill. Lymm. Mr. Hetherington alleged that Hattml had not had sole occupation, as for 'a time his bed- room was shared by an assistant in claimant's late father's business.

Applicant said he could please himself whether he allowed anyone to use the room. He could have refused the other man access. The vote was granted. The Conservatives supported the, claim of Peter Booth, Booths Hill Farm, for it lodger vote.

Claimant was employed by his father and paid 6s. per week for rooms. , Mr. Hetherington alleged that the man did not receive wages, and claimant admitted that he had an interest in the farm. Aft. Hetherington : Then you are a joint occu- pier?_

Claimant: Perhaps so. He also added that be paid the 6s. out of the profits he made from the farm.

How Trades were Taught and Learned a CentelTy Ago.

SOME DOCUMENTS EIELATINCI TO OLD ifiItIPAiIiNGTONIANS.

Business life ran in different channels in the Warrington of seventy years ago. It flowed more slowly. , and with none of the feverish haste which marks its passage to-day. Though in some aspects less attractive, the Methods of the past have points of value and interest, and the turning over of old business papers gives many slight yet useful social side lights.

We need only reach seventk years back to feel that we are almost before the days of banks and postage, and that the days of the registered letter were a long way off. .It is the carrier who brings money from outlying districts, accom- panied by letters such as this :—. Honoured Sir,— I send to you by favour of the Carrier - Ten Pounds in cash. I have no further money at hand, but hope to send the.balanee by the same bearei. upon -next market day. Etc.

The carrier, whose horse. trotted into town on market days, was quite an institution, and he carried pistols in his box. It was upon market days that the town was really . alive. Then shopkeepers adorned the footpaths with samples of their goods for the admiration of the visitors who flocked the streets, and ,the inn yards were crowded to• the point of confusion. Yet even on market days tradespeople went "a nooning," and the dinner hour allowed its nap or gossip at the Green Dragon parlour.

And he, the said Apprentice, shall and will faithfully serve his said master, his secrets keep, his lawful commands obey and do; hurt to his said master he shall not do, nor suffer to be done by others, when it is in his power to prevent the same: His master's goods he shall not waste or em- bezzle, the same give or lend without leave; Day nor night absent himself from his said master's service; nor do any other Act, Matter or Thing whatsoever to the Preju- dice Of his said master, but in all Things shall demean and behave himself towards his master and all his as a faithful Appren-

tice ought to do. • The language `` werges on the poetical," as old Weller would have said, but becomes. solid and prosaic- enough when recording that the sum of ten pounds of good and lawful money is to go with the boy. ' In the matter of food and cloaths," the ,con- ditions are the same as in the earlier Inden- ture: save that the. boy is to have "two suits of cloaths, one of the said suits to be for Sunday and the other fOr work day," and "the said John Oldfield .shall and will turn out the said William Clarke at the end of the said term with two suits of cloaths, one of the paid suits to be for Sundays and the other suit for work days." This document, is signed by John Haddock, printer, and successor to William Eyres, who acknowledges His Majesty's duty 8f five shil- lings.

HOW CHARITY CHILDREN WERE TRAINED. .In ,yet another Indenture the churchwarden and overseer of Winwick hands over "John 'Baker, a poor boy, belonging to the said town- ship, of the age of eleven years or thereabouts," as an apprentice to John Lear, "with him to cohabit and dwell after the manner of an

ipprentiee for and during the term of eight rear."

The boy is to learn the business of tailor and

the churChwafden and overseer pay with him a sum of seven guineas. The terms are a little more elaborate, in which is forbidden,

At dice, cards or any unlawful game he shall not play or exercise himself therein; ale-houses, taverns, evil company he shall not frequent; Fornication or Adultery be shall not commit, nor matrimony contract during the said term. The date is February, 1804. In another, of

1762, Ellen Smyth, "a poor girl, aged twelve years," is apprenticed to John Moss for three years, to be instructed in the occupation of "spinster."

We are told that it was customary in War- rington for the relatives of the apprentice, to stipulate that salmon should not be given as food On more than two or three days in any one week, the explanation being .that salmon were so plentiful in the Mersey, and conse- quently SQ cheap, that they appeared upon the table sufficiently often to nauseate even a grow- ing and hungry apprentice. But no Indenture In the bunch before us has any stipulation as to the kind of food.

The completion of an apprenticeship was a period of importance and jubilation, and where the status of the apprentice allowed,. a supper party was held, his Indenture,. duly signed ,by the master, was handed to him, and there were speeches.

One of these is before us, and contains the endorsement, "Joseph Mounfield as serve is time and as been a faithful apprentice, Robert

Bradley.", The said Robert probably knew more of his trade than of the King's English, but the testimonial .would be prized, grammar notwithstanding.

THE APPRENTICE SYSTEM.

Among many changes in business life, we seleFt one for notice in this brief article. In those days the apprentice system was in full- est sway. The youth who was to learn ,a trade was bound by a legal document to the service of an employer. Usually he lived with the employer in his own home, shared. his table, as well as workshop, and was provided with clothing. The employer, on his part, undertook to teach, clothe, and feed the appren- tice, receiving in return his labour for a term of years, and in early times a payment as well. Large employers had often half a dozen appren- tices at their table, in addition to their own family, and meal times were times indeed. Perhaps our readers will be interested to read one of the legal documents under which the was made. The Indenture quoted is rrnaontget earliest, but we choose it because it ariee rit Is endorsed by William Eyres, who writes: "Received, one shilling and sevenpence, his Majesty's duty, on the within Indentures, according to statute. W. Eyres." This Indenture, of Three parts, made the 29th day of September, in the year 1778, between Mary Layer, of Appleton; John Layer (son of the said Mary Layer), and Joseph Ellam, of Moore, Taylor. Witnesseth that the said John Layer, of his own free will and with the consent and approbation of the said Mary Layer, hath placed and bound and cloth put, place and bind himself an apprentice and covenant servant to and with the said Joseph Ellam, with him to cohabit and dwell as such, from the day of the date hereof and for and dur- ing and unto the full end and term of seven whole years ensuing, and fully to be corn- pleat a,nd ended. During all the which time the said ap- prentice his said master well and faithfully shall serve, his secrets keep, his and their commands lawful and honest at all times willingly do, and obey in all things, demean and behave himself towards his said master and all his and their family, as becometh a faithful apprentice and covenant servant. In consideration whereof, and of the sum of one pound eleven shillings and sixpence by the said Mary Layer . . . the said Joseph Ellam shall at all times during the said term well and sufficiently employ, teach and instruct the said apprentice in the Art, Trade, Business and Employment of a Taylor, which he now useth and followeth, to the best of his skill and judgment, as much as thereto belongeth, and the capacity of the said apprentice will allow of. And also shall and will, at his own xpenee not only find for the said apprentice good wholesome and sufficient Meat, Drink, Washing and Lodging from time to time and at all times . . . but also all and all manner of good„,rhole, decent and suit- able cloaths and wearing apparel of all kinds during the four last years of the said term, and likewise keep his apparel well and sufficiently repaired and mended during the three last years of the term aforesaid. And the said Mary Layer will at her own xpence provide good, whole, decent and suitable cloaths of all kinds at all times during the three first years of the said term, repairing and mending the same only xcepted. Etc., etc. The document is duly witnessed and stamp duty paid at the rate of sixpence per poupcl upon the premium paid. Another Indenture is that by which William

THE TRICKS OF THIEVES.

The hiding-places resorted to by thieves for the bestowal of their booty are ()Rep excessively ingenious, but not so ingenious as to bailie the wits of a Sherlock Holmes. That gentleman has his counterparts in Germany, ag a corre- spondent of the "hamburger Nachriehten " shows plainly enough. • "A detective was once on the track of some ,precious stones which on, a railway a thief had , abstrated from a lady's bag. A lady-love who had been deserted by the thief and wished to be revenged on him, infornied •me that the stones had been lodged in the keeping of a cousin, a well-to-do peasant. An examination of his house proved fruitless, but as the detective was leaving he had to pass a horse harnessed to a plough which was standing in the courtyard. "Take care! The horse kicks !" cried out the peasant. , This struck the detective as rather strange, because his way did not lead him close past the horse. So he made trial of the beast to see whether it did really kick, and fouiid it was as quiet as a lamb. Then he examined the harness, and in the collar were the stolen stones. _

"At the end of 1910 I Was the guest of a wealthy lord of a manor. In the course of conversation his wife told me that some Iveeks before her husband had suffered a severe loss. Sixteen thousand-mark notes had disappeared in a wholly inexplicable manner from a drawer in a writing table; the search made, for them had proved quite fruitless. "In the evening I was playing with the three children of my host; they brought their play- things to show me, and amongst them were three huge Teddy bears. As I wanted a lar monster for my little girl 1 looked at them

closely. Seeing this, the mother came up and said, with a smile: You mustn't make our little daughter jealous with your attentions to her Pummelchen,' the name of the Teddy, bear. Just as I was in, the act of restoring the bear to the child the governess joined us. She threw a waspish look at me, and, in the rudest manner possible, seized the bear from my hands. "Hub! my young lady,: there's something wrong about that,' Hashed through my brain. On some pretext or other I got the bear back into my hands_ again, took a knife from the table, and in the twinkling of an eye Teddy was In two halves, and there amongst the stuffing were the notes;

"The cashier of a Hungarian bank disap- peared with £75,000. He was caught and sent to prison. After his release he lived for some time in Vienna, then he went to the Riviera, and settled down in a small house there. He had a perfect passion for butterflies; hundreds of cases adorned the walls of his rooms. At his death only a small sum of money. was found, and his heirs, angry at this discovery, proceeded to knock the butterfly cases to pieces; when two banknotes fluttered to the ground. It appeared that all his ill-gotten gains had been stowed away under the cork slabs of the cases:

"Very original was the manner in which a bank 'messenger, who has just been let out of prison, kept his money. He sent it under a false name to a foreign bank, and had the re- ceipt made out on a peculiarly thin and tough paper. With this he went to a surgeon, who cut his scalp, lifted up a flap of skip, put the receipt ,underneath, and Sewed up the wound. When the wound had healed up, the man fell into the hands of the police and served his time. After his release he had the Wound reonened, took.out the-receipt, and got his money."

THE " OUTDOOR " APPRENTICE.

This Indenture is for what was known as "outdoor apprenticeship," that is, it governs an arrangement by Which the youth lived at home and not at his employer's table. It recites that Joseph Mounfield, with the con- sent of his father, John Mounfield, "cloth put, place and bind himself a covenant servant or apprentice to the said Robert Bradley during the term of seven years, his father providing meat, drink, washing and lodging, and all necessaries: And also all kinds of, tools neces- sary for the said apprentice during the said term."

On the other side, -" in consideration of the faithful services of the apprentice, the employer agrees to tench, inform and .instruct by the best Ways and Means he can in the Trade or busi- ness of a Cabinet Maker." Wages are to be paid to the lad's father for his use upon the following scale: "One shilling

weekly, and for every week for and as his hire or wages, the slim of two shillings during the second year, the sum of three shillings dur- ing the third year," and so on until the seventh year, 'during which seven shillings per week is paid. The remuneration appears small, but it has to be remembered That the .wages of a mature joiner or cabinet maker would not at that period be more than fifteen shillings a week. The indoor apprentice system bad value, as it had drawbacks, but whether for gain or loss, it has become a thing of the past. It is one of trie 'bygones of our commercial life.

- • - And since no one expects its revival, we have treated it as an antiquity, and given here these few scraps of information.

The World's Tribute of Interesting

Items of News.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

It is not probable that there are many places in which the curfew bell is still rung uwery night.

Tenby is a town which still adheres to the old custom, but the office of ringer is nr vacant. A few days ago Miss Mary Noot, the old lady who for many years performed that office in addition to being caretaker of the Town Hall, passed away -at the age of seventy-eight.

Butterfly Farming.

There exists at least one farm in England for the sole purpose of raising butterflies. It is at Bexley, in Kent, and on an acre of land enor- mous quantities of lepidoptera are bred—the owner keeps a stock ,of over a hundred thousand. The calling is not without its romance and interest. Butterflies are often so local that some species • are only met with in one field in a single English county, and the " farmer" has to spray his flowers with honey and water owing to the absence of sufficient natural honey to feed [hi butterflies.

How Ignorance Hampers Science.

The project of running a geodetic baseline between Cairo and the Cape of Good Hope calls attention to the strange hostility often shown by savage tribes' to the operations of engineers. In India it has been found that the erection of

pillars and cairns t 6 mark the site of surveying, stations almost invariably attracts the atten- Hen of natives in the vicinity, who subsequently destroy the monuments. Similar trouble is found in Africa, South America and elsewhere.

Diet and Nationality.

Among modern nations the greatest eaters are the British, Germans, French and Ameri- cans—the ruling people'of our civilisation. The diet of the Spaniards and the Italians is notably less substantial ,than that of the British and Germans, just as their brains are less active and original. The Americans are, on the aver- age, the greatest eaters in the world. Said Carlyle to Emerson, "The best thing I know of that country is that in it a man can have meat for his labour."

Our Filthy Railway Carriages.

Sanitary arrangements on our railways are the subject of caustic criticism in a report by tle.borough medical officer of Deptford to the Fiublic Health Committee. On the cleaning of the carriages the report states: "Anybody who has seen the prehistoric method, of carriage cleaning at present employed in this country, which chiefly consists:‘of violently disturbing the microbe-laden dust on the floors and allow- ing it to settle on the cushions, must often have wished to have the authority to teach the cleaners how to go to work:'

Surrey Tobacco:

To the list of British counties where tobacco is cultivated, the name of Surrey must now be added. An eight-acre ,plot at Byfleet bears a crop that will shortly be ripe for gathering, and from which great things are expected. People who are sceptical as to the cultivation of tobacco in these islands should polish up their history. The fragrant herb was at one time responsible for a flourishing British industry, eventually killed by Government orders. His- tory may well repeat itself so far as the success of the industry is concerned, but not, we hope, in the matter of its suppression.

The Largest Paddle Boat.

There is running on Lake Erie for the first time this season, a, gigantic paddle-whe9l steamer, built to the order of the Cleveland and Buffalo Transit Company. This vessel, says the "hallway and Travel Monthly," is not only the largest paddrle7,wheel steamer. in the, world, but is unlikely.,tO,have, its glory dimin- ished by any rival, since, for technical reasons, her designer has announced .that he will never project a larger. The boat has a length of 500 feet, which, considering that she IS built for lake traffic, contrasts favourably with the Mauretania's 762. She carries 5,000 passen- gers. •

Putting Trees to Bed.

An. interesting method of protecting peach trees from frost during the winter has been practised for several years at the Agricultural Experiment Station at Canyon, Colorado. Early in November the earth is removed from a circle about four feet in diameter round each tree, and water is turned in to asturate the soil. When the ground has become soft the tree is worked back and forth to loosen the roots, and Is then pushed over on its side. The branches are brought together and fastened with a cord, and burlap covered with earth is put over them. Thus the trees lie snug .until spring, when the covering is gradually loosened and finally re- Moved, and they are raised and propped up.

Isolation Hospital With Glass Wards.

Individual glass rooms, with private tele- phones, are to be provided ,for patients recover.; lag from infectious diseases in a new isolation hospital in Chicago. Instead of tieing grouped together in a general Ward, the patients will have private rooms, separated by an air-tight glass partitiap from a public corridor, so that their friends can see them and talk with them by telephone without exposing themselves to infection. This is an advance over Vie present system which prohibits friends from. seeing or receiving- letters from 'convalescing patients. The ward buildings of the new isolation hospi- tal will be built in circular form around the administration building, and arrangements are to be made for handling all infectious disease except smallpox.

Paris Nurse Revives Loves Philtres.

Mme. Poirson, a trained nurse, claims to have discovered, or rediscovered, "magic" charms in perfumes which may .be used. as love phi!- tees. She says she has studied perfumes and their influences for many years, and adds: Per- fumes have many qualities and are of sovereign value to both sexes. For instance, lavender has a great use.. If one Wants to influence an- other person all that is necessary is, to present, lavender to him and then sit down quietly and think hard on the subject. The result is cer- tain. Lavender is especially efficacious in the case of military men and lovers. "The frd- grant ambergris has irresistible attractions for Persons who enjoy life and for expansive per- sons. It elevates the senses, and is the only love talisman which overcomes the evil influ- ence of a jealous and envious rival. The scent of musk harmonises with fiery . temperaments. One may dare anything when under the influ- ence of the magic verbena plant's, which Was sacred to Druids. It is in great demand by fashicinable Parisiennes as a love philtre." . A

WHAT GOES ON EVERYWHERE.

An American paper publishes the following verses descriptive of mad malsing and destruc- tion which goes on in the States, and of which English ratepayers have also some experience :

They took a little gravel, And they took a little tar, With various ingredients Imported from afar;

They hammered It, and rolled it, And when they went away,

They said they had a pavement That would last for many a day.

But_ they came with picks and smote it, To lay a water main, And then they called the workmen TO put it back again.

To run a railway cable They took it up once more, And then they put it back again, Just where it was before.

They took it up for conduit, To run the telephone, And then they put it back etgin, As hard as any stone.

They took it up for wires, To feed the lectric light, And then they put it back again, Which was no more than right.

Oh! the pavements full of furrows, There are patches everywhere, You'd like to ride upon it, But it's seldom that you dare.

It's a very handsome pavement, A credit to the town; They's always 'diggin' of it up, Or puffin' of it down.

A THEATRE RUN BY WOMEN.

In Paris there is a theatre, known as the Ba-ta-Clan, which is unique, since it is run by a woman, Mme. B. li*asimi, and is managed entirely, by women. It is 15 years now since madame bought the theatre, being led to do so first by need to work, then by her love of colour and her feelings for threatrical affairs. In three years she had made a success of her ven- ture, and, as she herself says, "I do not see how a man could have done better wh illy theatre than I have, and I, cannot believe that he would have earned more than my revenue of last year, viz., £24,000!" And this success has been obtained, not by grinding down her employes, but by paying them the best prices. Her artistes and workers stay with her and do the best they can for her, since they know that madame will make the best arrangements she can for them. The wors; is very hard, though, and for a fortnight before any new production, no one, as she herself says, "must speak to me or come near me except on business concerned with my theatrical affairs." She is an embodiment of the saying that "Genius is an infinite capacity for taking pains" —as every detail, however minute, goes through her own hands, and is inspected by her, nothing being too trivial for her personal consideration. Every costume is made in the theatre by women:- Madame buys all her silks and satins, as well as the other fabrics, herself, and they must all be of good quality in order that she may get a good colour scheme. She buys them in their, original white, and then has them dyed to her orders. .Every other detail re- quired is made at the theatre, wigs, tights, em- broidery, artificial flowers, decorations, Sze., and, in addition, when any new production is in hand, there is an immense amount of work to be done studying old prints and pictures and documents in order to have everything as per- fect as possible. But with it all madame is the true type of French Woman, for her hobbies outside the theatre are her home and her children; and her words on this point are worth quoting: "It is my conviction that however successful a woman may be, however superior she may prove her- self in her chosen career, she will gain nothing by neglecting her home; or deputing her duties to her children."

New Books.

E PACIFIC DEVELOPMENTS OF BRITISH CIVILISATION.

Pax Britannica, a study of the tiistory of British Pacification. By 1-1. S. Perris, M.A. Lon- don: Messrs. Sidgwick and Jackson, Ltd., 3, Adam-street, Adelphi, W.C. Price ss. net.

The author of this book (who, by the way; we are glad to recognise is, in some measure, related to Warrington, he having received some portion of .his early . education in: the town, we believe) • could not haVe chosen a more fitting time for its publication. Now, when so many absorbing questions are agitating the public mind, all bearing on the much-needed and long- desired pacification of those branches of our family nationalities which, for generations, have chafed under the repressive rule .01 institutions —forced upon them and productive of nothing but dissension—it is essentially necessary .that clear views should be imparted to those who are called upon to form a judgment as to the justice of the measures which are being taken to effect that end.

orne Rule for Ireland is still in the balance,

if we Ore to regard the present irreconcilable attitude of the Ulsterites as seriously meant to be kept up to the point of revolt. The appease-

lent Of Nonconformist Wales by the Disestab- ishment of the Church, in the Principality, is till hotly opposed by the clerical party, though

heir contention that it, is, or ever has been, in ,ny 'sense, ,the National Church of Wales, ap- wars ridiculous. Other important develop- 'lents, in the industrial and social life of the

eople, in all quarters under British rule, make

• . it more and more desirable that a clear under- standing should be reached, and opinions formed, with a full knowledge of the history of the times in which conditions came. into being that it is now sought to ameliorate. And for

is reason we welcome this study of the evolu-

tion Of the pacific element in our civilisation, which Mr. Perris offers, and would very earn- estly recommend its careful perusal by all who are wishful to know the actual facts of the history of British Pacification. Mr. Perris's object and aim has been '‘,ko trace the ever-advancing triumph of order over anarchy—of tolerance and friendly co-opera- , tion over provincial hatreds and sectarian bigotry throughout all the ages of British History ; and whatever may be our reflections on the trend of present-day opinion regarding our supposed duty to be prepared for war with other nations, as well as for the defence of our own country, we Must acknowledge that this book furnishes us with convincing proof of the steady advance of a peaceful inclination and an increasing readiness to submit differences to independent arbitration. And it is surely good that we should lean to the side of optimism in the pursuit of such a noble aim. A confi- dent ontlook towards peace and gogd-will, and constant effort for their attainmnt, must, assuredly, result in the accomplishment, ulti- mately, of the desired end. The ideal may not be realised in our day, but that should be no bar to the putting forth of our utmost power in

the endeavour to- assist forward the beneficent movement. As Tennyson sings, in one of his most inspiring lyrics We sleep and wake and sleep, but all things move And human things, returning on themselves, move onwards

. . seas that daily gain upon the, shore, Have ebb and flow conditioning their march, And slow and sure comes up the golden year.

•. . . . and well I know, That unto him who works and feels he works, This same grand year is ever at the doors. It would be a pleasure to quote largely from Mr. Perris's book, but we forbear.. He has a most attractive style and displays. a „perfect genins. for .mat slialling facts in succinct form and lifting into prominence the salient pOints in the history which reflect the actions and con- duct of the workers of the country, as con- trasted with those, of the aristocratic caste, at whose instigation, and for whose' benefit, most of the wars which desolated the lands of these islands were entered upon at various periods. We commend this really valuable addition to our historical .literature to the notice, particu- larly, of the younger % generation of politicians, to whom, we are sure, it will serve as an inspiration. We feel we cannot close this rather brief notice without letting Mr. Perris be heard in his own compelling language from a passage dealing with Anglo-American relations. He says :

It is to Great Britain and America that the

friends of peace look With hope and confidence. I For in both the spirit of democracy has awak- ened—an enlightened democracy, which is learning that militarism is onc of the greatest of the evils that aftict mankind. In both, a profound love of justice and a genuine humani- tarian passion survives the taints of blood and the temptations of wealth and power. The best minds both of England and America are increasingly pre-occupied with the task of organising the world's life on the basis of mutual help and forbearance, and substituting the rule of law for the misrule of war. In both countries the familiar doctrines of I physical force are on their defence, and, though still powerful, are no longer entirely in the ascend- ant. In the inspiring crusade against war, it is of good presage for the world that America and England lead the way."

Literary Gleanings.

UNTHANK HALL. Bishop Ridley's Birthplace.

By CHARLOTTE MASON, Ifl "Christian Service.".

Not far from " Bardon " Mill is the hamlet of " Unthank," which has interesting associations with Bishop Ridley., who was born at the Hall, about the year 1500—a fine, old family mansion, with its many' windows twinkling cheerily in the sunshine, and retaining to this .day an ancient chamber called the "Bishop's Room." The house is beautifully embosomed in woods with the wide purple moorlands of PlenmellOr stretching away behind it. Time seems to have stood still since the Bidleys from this .hamlet and from Willimotes‘vick Castle espoused the cause of Charles 1., and by their adherence to the King in 1652, forfeited their estates. The place' itself is like a bit of another century, dropped down into this; Ridley, the venerable bishop and martyr—who suffered at the stake with his friend Latimer—was the son of Chris- topher Ridley and grandson of Nicholas Ridley; of Willimoteswick Castle, now a ruined fortress picturesquely covered with yellow lichens. This old castle disputes with Unthank Hall the honour of being his birthplace. The Martyrs' -memorial in Oxford, erected over the spot where Crahmer, Ridley and Latimer suffered, marks, as it were, "The spiritual centre ,of England."

PREPARING THE GUNPOWDER PLOT.

In October, 1605, the Gunpowder Plot was afoot, and one of the noble conspirators, Sir Everard' Dighy, of Gothurst, was seeking a safe place in which his young wife and family might shelter during the corning events: He came to an . arrangement with Mr. Throckmor- ton to use a manor bouse called Coughton, near Alcester. Throckmorton was a Catholic, and, of course, sympathised with the plot. The reason, of this change of residence was that Gothurst, lying between the little rebel army, at Dunchurch and London, was soon likely to be visited by friends of King James. . The famous Jesuit, Father Garnet, was then in hiding at Coughton. The .place was noted for secret chambers, and being in those days a square house with a centre courtyard, was deemed capable of defence by a resolute body of then. A great mass of weapons and armour

===== 0002601_19130920_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
WRONGLY-NAMED SUBSTANCES.

Black lead does not contain a single particle of black lead, being composed of graphite. Brazilian grass does not come from Brazil, or even grow there; nor is it grass at all. It' is manufactured from strips of palm leaf (Cham- cerops argentea) and is ,imported chiefly from Cuba.

Burgundy pitch is not pitch, nor is it manu- factured in, or imported from Burgundy. The best is a resinous substance prepared from com- mon frankincense and brought from Hamburg; lciut by far the greater quantity is a mixture of palm oil and resin. Cuttle bone is not bone, but a structure of pure chalk, once embodied loosely in all the substance of certain extinct species of cuttle- fish. It is enclosed in a membranous sac, with the body`of the fish, and drops out when the sac is opened, but it has no connection what- ever with the sic of the cuttlefish. Galvanised iron is not galvanised. It is simply coated with zinc; and this is done by dipping ' it in a zinc bath containing muriatic acid. German silver is not silver, but a metallic alloy, which was not even invented by a ,Ger- man. It. has- been used in China , for ages. Honey soap contains no honey, but is one- part palm-oil soap and three-parts yellow or crude soap, scented.

Japan lacquer contains no lac and is made from a kind of nut itree. Meerschaum is ,a composition of silicia, mag- nesia, and water. The name implies petrified sea foam.

Mosaic gold has no conection with Moses or the metallic gold. It is an alloy of copper and zinc, used in the ancient musivum or tessellated work.

Mother-of-pearl is the inner of several sorts of shells, but not the real mother-of-pearl, rather being the matrix of pearl. Pen means a feather fLatin penna). A steel pen then is a misnomer. Salad oil is not oil for salad, but for cleansing salades.

Whalebone does, not possess any of the pro- perties of bone, but is a substance attached to the upper jaw of the whale, and serves to strain the water which the creatures takes up in large mouthfuls.

WE ARE GROWING STEADILY.

My Dear Children,— am, beginning, to feel a big uncle already. if I get new members, coming in as steadily as they" have done- I shall grow to a tremendous size, and will have to walk in the

horse road, as the footpaths will be too narrow, and if they do not get the work of widening the Bridge done quickly, and I keep growing a big uncle, I do not know how I shall get across in order to go home for my dinner. It iS very pleasant to find so many of you taking an interest in this column, and I am pleased to see you like it. At the risk of grow- ing ,so big that I cannot get across the bridge, I want to get many more nephews and nieces.

I would like to have every boy and girl in War- rington, Stockton. Heath, Lymm, and all the other places, members of our club. Am I not very greedy? But if you help me, I think we shall soon have a large membership. Then .we shall get a nice little badge for you to wear, so that you will know each other ns cousins, and the people, when they see you, will say, "My word, Uncle Jack has a very big family." To mark the fact that Niece Maimie Bayne was our first member, the founder of our club, in fact, I sent her a book, so that in years to 'come she can look at it and remember that she was my first member. She has sent me a nice little letter, which I publish below.

Now, how are you getting on with the com- petition? One nephew has already sent in his, and I am hoping to receive a lot from others by Saturday. Don't forget to send in your letter.

Your loving UNCLE JACK.

NIECE MAIAIIE BAYNE'S LETTER.

In acknowledging a gift of a book to cele brate the fact that she was our first member, Niece Mainlie Bayne writes: 12, Warburton-street, Stockton Heath, Sept. 10th, 1913.

Dear Uncle Jack,—Thanks very much for the mice book which I got this afternoon. I 'have read one tale, and like it very much indeed. Your loving Niece, NiAIIIIE BAYNE.

NEW MEMBERS. • Dear Uncle Jack,—Will you kindly enrol me as a- member in your big book. - BENNY HAMMON, 135, KNUTSFORD-ROAD, LATCHFORD.

Dear Uncle Jack,—Will you please enter my name in your big book.

FIILDA PALMER, 3, WORSLEY-STREET, WARRINGTON.

The . two new members have been duly en- rolled, and I am sure we all give our new cousins a most hearty welcome to our club. U. J.

Spilt Milk and Spoilt Clothes.

A clergyman recently engaged a boy to attend to his pony and make himself generally useful.

One of his duties was to milk a cow, but the latter, not liking the lad's clumsy method, quickly upset him, with the Vail of milk on top of him.

The minister, appearing on the scene and finding the boy in tears, said to him : "Never mind, my boy, Ws no use crying over spilt milk." " Aa's not crying Ower your auld milk," the boy replied, "but just look at me claes I"

A Hoax Museum.

The following is an interesting way of pro- viding a " hoax " museum. You can call it the " Do'em Art Gallery." Write out a pro- gramme containing the names of your "pic- tures?' The following will suggest subjects: (1) The Late King E!_lward, (2) Interior. of Wind- sor, (3) Fire of Warrington, (4) The Cas‘aways, (5) Result of a Strike, (6) Sweet Seventeen, (7) An Old Organ-blower, (8) The Grub that makes the Butterfly. As you will guess, it is not al real show of pictures. No. lis a King Edward Id. stamp; No. 2, a piece of Windsor soap cut in two pieces; No. 3, a piece of coal; No. 4, two cigar ends; a lot of struck matches is No. 5, and seventeen lumps of sugar is No. 6, an old red handkerchief is No. 7, the eighth is, a piece of dry bread. Any inventive boy or girl can easily increase the number of the subjects.

The Boy Who Dared.

One day in the middle of winter, as an old -man was walking along by a frozen pond, he stopped near two schoolboys, who were busy chattering to each other. "Fm glad the pond is frozen at last," said one of the boys, as he buckled on his skates. "You had better not truSt the pond to-day, my lad," said the old man, "it isn't very safe."

"What do you know about ponds being safe, old man?" said the boy, scornfully. "I am older than you, my boy," replied the old man, gently, "and I should-" "Bother!" exclaimed the boy impatiently, "are you ready, Carr?" "No, Tom:" answered Carr, "I aril not com- ing, for my mother said that I was not to skate if the pond wasn't safe." "It's perfectly safe," argued Tom. "I'm not coming," said Carr, decidedly. "Oh, you are going to believe all that old man says about ponds, and SOOII. Very well, then,, I'm off," and away he went. "I'm glad to see you take an old man's ad- vice," said the old man, turning to Carr, "for you are sure to itrofit by it, and " But he stopped suddenly, for Tom, who had been skating towards the middle of the pond, had disappeared, and nothing but a huge hole was left in the ice.

There was no help near, end, taking on old oar .that was lying on the ground, Carr and the old man crept along the ice as quickly as they could, until they came to the hole, then, lying out full length, they waited. But Tom never came to the surface again, and there were only Carr and the old man left to tell the sad tale of the disregarded advice. ,

The Family Cat. I can fold up my claws In my soft velvet paws, And purr in the sun Till the short day is done— For I am the family cat. I can doze by the hour, In the vine-covered bower, Winking and blinking Through sunshine and shower.

In the cold winter's night, When the ground is all white, And the icicles shine In a long silver line, I stay not to shiver In the moonbeams' pale quiver,. But curl up in the house As snug as a mouse, And play Jacky Horner In the coziest corner, Breaking nobody's laws With my chin on my paws. Asleep with one eye and awake with the

other, For pats from the children, kind words from the mother, For I am the family cat.

Some iliildles.

Why may not the proprietor of a forest fell his own trees?—Because no one is allowed to cut when it is his own deal. Why may a beggar wear a very short coal? —Because it will be long enough before he gets another.

Why is a bad picture like weak tea?—Be- cause it is not well drawn.

ATTRACTIVE ADDRESSES.

There were doubtless several causes respon- sible for the record gatherings at the meetings of the Warrington and District Baptist Union, held at Little Leigh, one of which may be reasonably set fortn as the prolonged spell of fine weaftier, making an excursion to one ,of the village churches such a pleasant 'experienCe, another being the provisiOn, of •so .attractive a programme as was 'arranged for .on this occa- sion.

After the delights experienced during. an hour's • ride on the top Of a 'bus along the Che- shire roads, and the temptation to extend the journey by rambling further, it was by no means a difficult task to persuade the people to enter the precincts of the little chapel, which has stood for the best part of a Century for Atte advocacy of Free Church principles, and has again and again echoed with the voices of those Who have faithfully served the -denomination, of whose. principles .they .are 'so proud, for .on this occasion there was a mime which never fails- to arouse expectations in the minds of those. who know the Man behind the name. -

The proceedings were to commence by a paper by Mr. E. 'Wynne Jones on the subject "Our Denominational Position as Baptists." The paper proved to be of a very 'interesting nature. It certainly opened the avenue for dis- .cussion and criticism, which were freely in- dulged in. At its conclusion the Rev. J. S. Chedburn, president, thanked Mr. Jones for the paper, after which he replied to his critics in his characteristically able manner., The accommodation of the school proved al- together inadequate when tea time came round, and a large number were under the necessity of exercising their patience, and awaiting what is known as the "second sitting:" -Owing to this it was•time for the public meeting to com- mence as soon as tea was dispensed with. •

The Palk Meeting.

This meeting was presided over by Mr. B. Few, vice-president of the Union. After prayer by the Bev. George Curtis, the Bev. Alexander" Harvey, of Widnes, .made a brief but touching reference ,to the life and work of the late Mr. W. Arnold, who had been connected with the Union for a number of years, and who, had life and health been spared, would have been present to address the meeting. His tribute to the nobility of character and disposition, to the entire abandonment of himself in the work to which. he set • himself, to his faithfulness as a man and a minister, found a warm response in. every heart, for to know Mr. Arnold was to prove all these things and infinitely more. His proposal fo send from that meeting a. message of sincere sympathy and condolence Lo the church at Golborne, who hild lost po con- scientious and devoted a pastor. and to the wife and child who had lost so true and ideal a husband and. father, Was met with ready accord attil Confirined--bY all standing.

After the singing of a hymn, the Rev. E. E. Lovell,- of the Higher •Lane • Baptist ''Church, Lymm, delivered an address. This was Mr. Lovell's first appearance away from his 'own church since his settlement in Lymm. but apart from his own genial personality, his uplifting and inspiriting addres4 will ensure for him an exceeding warm welcome on any future occa- sion he may find himself confronting a Baptist audience. .

===== 0002601_19130920_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
An Esteemed Speaker.

The other speaker bore a name esteemed amongst Ba:; lists generally, and tenderly re- garded and honoured by all living in that part of Cheshire; Nfiss Edith Aldis, daughter of the late Rev. John ,Aldis, who for a number • of years occupied the pastorate of Little Leigh Church. Miss Aldis, who is a speaker of out- standing ability, and has a most winning man- ner. delivered a very happy and helpful address, choosing as her übject "Little children, I have written to you because ye know the Father." Her message • Was based more particularly on the closing words, and constituted a call upon her hearers to come into closer. knowledge and experience of God as their. Father. At the close of the meeting the Secretary made an important announcement to the effect that the churches in the Union were combin- ing to hold a united meeting at Warrington on Monday, October 13, in aid of the great de- nominational enterprise known as the Rapti. Union Sustentation Fund an4d Ministerial Sof - tlement Scheme.

A CONTINENTAL HOLIDAY.

From Liverpool to the Field of

Waterloo.

A FULL SEVEN DAYS.

A correspondent writesi— I am writing this article describing my holi- day, three days' sailing to Antwerp and four days' touring in Belgium, hoping it will be in- teresting and instructive to the young men in the town who have thought, as no doubt many have, that they would like to spend a holiday on the Continent. I booked my berth on one of the Cork Steamship Co.'s boats, sailing to Antwerp on Thursday, the 21st August. I took my lug- gage on board in the afternoon, and had a look round the ship, which was beautifully fitted up. VACANT CHAIRS.

The following morning I was up on deck about seven o'clock, but the weather was rough and it was raining. I went down to break- fast at 8.30 : there were several vacant seats. The captain, a jovial chap, kept us alive, relat- ing anecdotes, many of which had to be taken with salt. Dinner was served at one o'clock (some passengers still missing). Passing Car- digan Bay we sighted a battlesfilp and a sub- marine, which I saw quite distinctly through the glasses. Tea was served at six o'clock (I was missing). Saturday was a lovely day, rather dull before breakfast, but it soon brightened up, aha the sea was quite calm. We entered the English Chairel early ”L the morning, and about ten o'clobk we sighted two battleships that were out for gun practice. I heard the firing quite distinctly, and at first thought it was thunder, until the captain pointed out the spray caused by the shot. It was a fine sight through the glasses, just like a miniature waterspout. We had a pretty view while sailing past Ventnor, the lights along the shore giving a fine effect. Sunday morning we saw both England and France, the white cliff of Dover on one side and Calais on the other being seen quite plainly with the naked eye. The sail up the Scheldt was very interesting, the scenery very much so: We arrived in Antwerp on gunday evening at slx o'clock, bad dinner on board, and then took the car to the Zoological Gardens. The gardens are beautiful and well worth visiting!

===== 0002601_19130920_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SIGHTS OF BELGIUM

Monday I went to Brussels an changed for Ghent; spent the afternoon in the exhibition, had tea, and caught the 7.30 train to Ostend. Here the promenade is about three miles long, and at one end is the King's summer palace. Among the buildings are the Hotel de Ville and ss. Peter and Paul Church. The Kursaal is very grand, and is open from June to October, with a good restaurant attached to it. The symphonique Orchestra plays in the evenings. afterwards there is dancing in the beautiful Casino, besides theatrical performances and regattas. Bathing is very good and the sands excellent. _ _ _ _

--Tuesday I caught the midday train to Bruges, the Flemish Brugge (or bridges, of which there are about fifty short ones). Here six canals meet from Ostend, Ghent, Dunkirk, Sluys, Fumes and Ypres. in the large dock or basin. The Cathedral of .St. Sauveur, 4701 t. long and .90ft. high, is of Gothic brick, and rich in wood carving. There are .several good paintings, among which is the Crucifixion by Van Hoecks. The Gothic. Church of Notre Dame has a' tower of about 430 ft. high—marble of the Virgin and Child, supprzsed to be by Michael Angelo. Among the paintings are the Virgin and Child by Van Dyck, the Adoration of the' Shepherds, and 'many. others. The pulpit is a highly fin- ished carving in wood. The Hotel de Ville is a fine restored Gothic edifice of the 14th century, and its Chapel St. Sang is a Gothic gem. The Belfry of Bruges is 3501 t. high. The Halles in Grand ,Place, has a celebrated chime of bells, which Can be inspected by paying a small fee. Lace working is seen in almost every street, and the old Market-place is very curious. Bruges has the characteristics of a Dutch town more than any other in Belgium, and is one of the most picturesque.

Wednesday I proceeded to Brussels, the capital of the kingdom, and the seat of the Gov- ernment. Among the plases of interest are the King's palace in the beautiful park, Hotel de Ville, a line Gothic pile or the 15th century, with a spire of 364 ft. high, from which Waterloo can be seen. Musee Wiertz contains some most extravagant productions, well worth seeing, among which are "Entering Paradise," "The Rosebud," "A Second after Death," and "The Dog in his Corner." The celebrated Porte de Hal, an ancient town gate, once a Bastille on a small scale, is nowt used, as a Museum of An- tiquities. Palais de Justice in flue de le Regena and the Square of Brussels; last and also least, though most important .and far famed, is the Mannikin fogntain..

===== 0002601_19130920_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
WATERLOO.

I spent the afternoon at Waterloo viewing a number of Wellington curios; also the Lion monument, from which an extensive view of the battlefield is obtained. I returned to Ant- werp in the evening and slept aboard that night. Thursday morning I visited St. P,aufs ,:hurch, which contains a representation of Mount Calvary, the Crucifixion, and the Ordeal of Purgatory, executed in a rough manner; which forms a contrast to the other works of art in the churdn. There are also some fine paintings by Rubens, "The Descent from the Cross," a magnificent altar piece; "The Works of Mercy," and last "The Scourging of L'firlst." I also visited the Steen Museum, which con- tains a great many interesting antiques. We set sail from Antwerp at noon and had a very nice passage home, deck games being in full swing. Passing the Smalls, we saw a schooner on the rocks, which had struck them a fortnight previous. We passed New Brighton about eight o'clock on Sunday morning, and landed in Queen's Dock, Liverpool, about nine o'clock. I should advise anyone touring on the Conti- nent to purchase a State Railway- contract, say, for five or ten days, as it saves a great deal of time and unnecessary delay.

RED SPOTS COVERED FACE.

High-street, Burnham-on-Crouch, Essex, Eng. —" The trouble started about six years ago on my face, just round the nose and chin, but it worked about until it got round my neck and behind my ears. My face was covered with red spots, and I was tormented always with it. First it was little, red, hard pimples, then they used to dry up and itch dreadfully and used to come off in thin white scales. 1 was afraid to wash, as they used to burn like fire and itch for hours after.

"I treated about three months, but it only got worse. The first I used of the Cuticura Soap and Ointment I got relief and knew it was the very thing I wanted. I used to bathe my face, i neck and oars n water as hot as could be borne throe or foilr times a day, and the Cutictira Ointment twice a day.. My face, neck and ears are quite clear from it now." (Signed) Mrs. Warnecke, Jan. 30, 1913. Cuticura Soap and Ointment are sold by chemists everywhere. A sample of. each with 32-p. book post-free from nearest depot. Address: Newbery, 27, Charter- house-square, London; IL Towns & Co., Sydney, N.S.W.; Lennon, Ltd., Cape Town; Muller, Maclean & Co., Calcutta and Bombay; or "Cuticura," Boston, U.S.A. Men who shave and shampoo with Cuticura Soap will' find it best for skin and scalp.

THE CYCLIST'S NOTE BOOK

Preparing for the Short Days and Long Nights. BY SILVER STREET.

PICKERING BRIDGE.

The Cheshire County Council has at last fin- ished the restoration of this bridge, and on Saturday, tho 2Gth, it was opened for traffic, after eight months' holiday. The old bow bridge had become absolutely rotten, and the woodwork has now been completely replaced, as also that of the swing bridge. For the Weaver Valley is particularly beautiful at and below that spot. Hemmed in on both sides by steep wooded banks, the most navigated river in England is not at all spoiled by the frequent passage of barges laden with chemicals, salt or china clay, and it is a favourite objective for boating parties from Rock Savage. Just at present the hedges, are 'loaded with blackberries, and tea is comfortably served at Mrs. Wilkin- son's cottage at the bridge, on the north side. The best way from Warrington (and there are eight ways) is over Acton Bridge and Acton Station to Crowton. The road to the right leads directly to our beauty spot, which is just nine miles from 'Warrington. Thirteen of the W.W.C.C. made it their afternoon's holiday place on Saturday.

LAMPS AND WICKS.

These are coming out again now, and the first thing to do is to empty out all the old oil, - put the wick in the fire, and start afresh. An interesting thing, and orid worth knowing about these useful accessories—l am. only dealing with oil lamps, ,and, don't want any more dealings with acetylenes—in fact, most captains prefer a set of oil lampists to acetylenes—is that a .lain!) made to take inch wick will not, burn any that. is narrower. I -can't say why, but suspect that oapillary action has something to do with it. And the tales -dealers tell us about the use- lessness. of ordinary house lamp wicks in -cycle lamps are good.: business, but not true.. Wick at 3d. a yard burns- juSt as -Well as the five pieces for 6d., made up neatly in cardboard boxes. • If it still refuses to burn, clean put all the soot from the roof of the lamp with an old brush; they are different from some of us town workers-1-they must have fresh air. Would that we had more.

PUNCTURES.

As ,soon as the. farmer has got the various , crops in, then commences the. cutting of the hedges. As an antidote to stray thorns—in olden days there- were -no stray thorns, they were all over the road, often placed there by the grinning farm hand for the fun of the thing—well, as •an antidote, we are offered puncturestops,. -solutions, recipes to make our own, and so on. But I am firmly convinced that the Only fluid thing that ought- to enter a cycle tyre , is air.' All others‘ are sure to do damage, sooner or .later, especially to old patches. In a local paper of last Saturday's date a recipe was given for making your own puncture stop of sornething like 12 ingredients, including -powdered rubber and aniline-nigro- sine. No, that is ,too much! rather have the puncture. That reminds me of a time, only two years after' the invfition of the pneumatic Lyre, when I. was riding two Dunlop tyres in the neighbourhood,9f.Morecainbe, and upended my cycle jUSt outside. a smithy. Tile good fellow watched the process' of repair tor 'a few minutes, and when the long wait came ,Whilst the solution dried, his pity overcame him and he went, into The; shop for something to help me with-. He 'soon came out with a red-hot iron bar. "Will this be of any use to you?"

JAPHET IN SEARCH OF A FLOWER.

The modern, tendency towards what is con- veniently called Nature study is resp-onsible for many funny stories that crop up each week in the papers. Here is a new one, because original and, true. In" Wayside and Wood- land Flowers," by ,ttWacil Step, is depicted a little bide 'labiate flower called skullcap. It looks rather- like a dead nettle, and a hurried Observer would pass'it by for &tell: But two of us ,Who are- companions in botanical study searched eVerywhere. for this skullcap!' At last we had the delight Of setting eyes on our first specimen in the New 'Forest, near Lynd- hurst, and accordingly ticked.it off in our text- book. Within a week of coming home we actually found one' little plant 'growing in the lane that runs 'past our house! Poking its pretty little fingers up year by year through the grass, there it is sure enough, and search as we like we cannot light on another specimen anywhere nearer to Warrington than in, Petty- pool Park, where it grows in abundance. Lamp time to-night, 7.16. Next Saturday, 7.0,'

LATCHFORD'S, PROSPECTS.

Latchford commence their matches next Sat- urday. The fixture list is the strongest in the history of the club, and as there has been a .most encouraging , influx of new members it should be possible to put two capable elevens In the field. KILLED WHILE AT PLAY

An inquest on, Edward Stanley (14), of 19, Elephant-lane, Thatto Heath, St. Helens, was held by Mr. Brighouse on Monday. The evi- dence showed that .on Friday evening the boy was playing ball with a companion named Ben Pye. Pye ran into one of the backyards. Stan- ley pursued him, and on finding the door fag- toned be began to cliffib over the wall. He put his weight on the large stone which crosses the doorway. when it tipped over and fell with the boy to the ground, killing him instantly. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and expressed the opinion' that the property was in a dangerous condition. The Coroner said 1?e would rn-rmunicate with •tlio corporation mauLtr. ,

Bennion again peeled, but two singles on the 19th and 20th ends and Smith was home—our congratulations.

A number of interesting games were played in connection with the Works Association on Saturday, and the draw for the final section will be made on the completion of the games next Saturday.

We are in receipt of the following circular from Mr. Patrick (the Association secretary), Cwohni. cnhe i In o meetingdoubt owillf hien te r Works s t o uArs sroecadi a teiros "A n: was held on July 14th, 1913, when the question of providing ourselves with recreation grounds and club premises of our 'own was discussed/and it was unanimously decided that each works in the town be canvassed to see how many work- ing men wore prepared to take up shares, also to become -members of ,the Association Club. With this end in view, the committee have had circulars printed, whereon intending -share- holders may state the number of .21 shares he is prepared to take tip (the shares may be ,paid in eight instalments of 2s. 6d.). As soon as the circulars are returned a general meeting of the working men of the town will be held in some -hall, to be presided over by the President, when the v,-holo matter will be brought forward and discussed before deciding upon anything definite. The Council -will be guided by the number of applicants as -to the advisability Of proceeding with -the scheme.". , This seems to promise ,something, and. we should like to see the venture meet with success. It will be styled the "Warrington Working Men's Recrention Club."

The semi-final games in the Warrington and District Association Individual Merit ,Competi- Hon are to be played to-day.

Crosfield's Recreation Club bowling section have had a successful season, for Mr. Harrison, the secretary, 4as worked most‘ assiduously to that end.

CROSFIELD'S WORKS INTER-DEPART-

MENTAL COMPETITION.

FINAL TIE.

On Saturday the final stage of the inter-de- partmental competition promoted by the bowl- ing section of Messrs. Crosfield's Recreation Club, was reached. A' large number of enthu- siasts assembled on the Hood-lane recreatioTl ground to witness the match between the Oil Plant and Mr. Williams' No. 1 teams. The competition has been remarkably successful, 46 teams, , representing about 290 players, having participated in the games.

The final resulted-in—a—well-deserved victory for the Oil Plant bowlers, the scores being :

- Oil Plant. Mr. Williams' No. 1. Handicap 14 Handicap 15 W Welsby (3) H Cook (1) CI Fogg (3) I Middleton (2) .... 15 T Roberts (4) 1? Smith (3) J Chesters (2)

99 81 , The winners become the holder for the year of a handsome challepge cup, presented by Cap- tain G. IL Crosfield. In addition, the members of the team receive gold watches, and the run- ners-up silver watches. .

In handing the cup to Mr. Middleton, the captain of the victorious team, Mr. George Dale said bowling was a great source of pleasure, and that recreation ground was a splendid thing for the workmen of Messrs. Crosfield. Such schemes ought tá be taken up more gener- ally by large concerns all over the country. Just as a man who wished to become a musical genius must have the gift. so he believed that a man might practise bowling until doomsday, but un- less he had a natural bent for the game .he would be a failure. He was glad to know that the inter-departmental competition had been fought in ,the true spirit Of British #sportsmanship. (Applause.) Mr. Middleton, in accepting the 'trophy, ex- pres3ed sympathy ;with the losers, • who had

A GREAT BOWLER.

Fearnhead Man Beats Manchester Crack.

NOTABLE PEAT BY CHARLES ROBERTS.

The prettily-situated bowling green attached to that well-known hostelry the Farmers' Arms, Fearnhead, has been the scene of many a Well- fought *match at bowls. The genial. landlord, Mr. Charles Roberts,- is himself. one of the crack bowlers of 'Warrington' and district, and only last season greatly distinguished himself in the great bowling. annul festival 'which is held at Blackpool—where the finest prolessional bowlers eitch year meet to contest. Out of up- wards of .1,000 players, he won round • alloy round, and was only defeated after running into' the eighth place. Reeently.-Mr. Charles Roberts and his brother James were pitted against two famous bowlers of Harpurhey, viz., Messrs. Shepherd and Dixon, and the match has been played off dur- ing the past two weeks, alternately, at Harpur- hey Bowling Green and the Farmers' Arms Bowling Green tit Fearnhead. The game was 61 'up. On their own- green. at Harpurhey, Shepherd and Dixon led by 31 .to 15, but many persons knowing the calibre of the Brothers Roberts on their own green, thought it possible that the arrears would be wiped off. , On the resumption of the play on the Fearn- head green; after a very tough struggle, how- ever, the Harpurhey bowlers 'managed to re- tain the greater portion of this lead, and eventu- ally won the match by 61 to 48.

As Some very fine bowling was exhibited by Mr. Shepherd on the one side and Mr. Charles .Roberts on the other, a match was arranged between these two on the respective greens. The, Match was commencefil. last Monday at Fearnhead, where Mr. Charles Roberts man- aged to gain a .lead of 12, the score standing 32-20. The concluding stage of the match was played at HarpurheY, and .after a most exciting match, in which some magnificent bowling took place—Shepherd at one time overhauling Mr. Raerts and leading by 41 to 39—Roberts man- aged' to regain the lead and eventually won the match, amid much excitement, by 12, the scores standing at the finish 61-49.

At the BlaCkpool gowling festival, which is now taking place, several Warrington bowlers are distinguishing Themielves. Mr. Charles Roberts, Mr. T. Davies and otherhave already von their preliminary round. .

PICKS OUT OF THE SCRUM.

Pithy Points About N.U. Games.

Warrington did it at Batley. It was quite a creditable win. The score should have been a taller one. Had Warrington played as they began. Broughton fear the visit to-day. They expect to meet a defeat. The Rangers are not going strong. "We're all going the same way home!" So sang a Warrington youth after Batley He nearly missed the train, however, When he got out at Huddersfield. •

REcOn%

Who was the ploughman in Warrington team? Who tried to plough the pitch with his nose? The ground was a bit hard, George. And how did the nose feel on Sunday? Ruddick rollicked behind the scrum. He was as happy as a schoolboy.. Still it Was not a nutting job, Was it, worthy friend, Ruddick? Bradshaw was in dashing form at Batley. Pity he did not get the ball more. If he had the score would have been larger. Your try was a gem, Bradshaw.

* *

Howard Davis was In tackling humour. He made a Batley man spin like a top. . One good turn deserved another, of course. Ruddick has -learnt a tip from Huddersfield. His the way to,bounce the ball into scrum. He has adroitly learned the Huddersfield art. And he mystified Batley men with it. Dickenson was an adept at finding touch. Perhaps he found it a little too often. But that was all in the nature of the game. Who said that Ruddick cannot 'run? Ile ran particularly well at Mount Pleasant.

*

Nicholas was always on the alert. lie fed the threequarters much better, The Batley crowd are rather biased. They do not like opponents to do anything. Batley, they think, should have all the 'cake. A 16-stone Batleyite charged Jolley. The little 'un stepped upon one side. Thud! went the elephant to earth. Batley spectators yelled in their anger. Of course, it was not fair, from their view point: Of Jolley not.allowing himself to be knocked over. Jolley just smiled joyfully at the incident..

* * * * * * Clare, with his speed, is a valuable man. His runs were like aeroplane dashes. His swerves might be caned vol-planes. Lyons said he didn't like Clare at all. The Batley back had to put up with it. Actually Brookes scored a try, and • He raced all through Batley to touch down. However, the whistle had sounded before then. Brookes didn't knoW-he had been offside. Ruddick had a similar experience later. - "Stop!" yelled the Batley eroWd ; Ruddick ran on.

-He thought the ,cry of " stop " was a specta- tors' dodge. -

-•• * *

"Kill 'ern," sqretuned the Batk.-Tites. This was when Smith got hurt. A youth on the stand kept shouting it. This annoyed a Batley journalist. He seized a plank and went to the youth. The "plank.c,arne down "wlick 1" The youngster was fairly caught behind. He ceased to yell in order to rub. The young man didn't like the tackle. However, he wisely said "nowt." It would have done no harm had some others Amongst the spectators got 'a siiiillar

* *

* * *

The old order changeth Warrington "A " are no longer afraid of Leigh-Shamrocks. Indeed, they are very " cocky " since Saturday Eleven points to three. Who'd ha' thowt it? What about Baker now? He knows how many beans make five, Although he doesn't come from Boston. He shone again on Saturday, _ And was as plucky as ever. He has plenty of dash, too, And perhaps a little temper. But that first try of Waywell's I * * * * * *

A beauty, wasn't it? How he ran. And how the crowd cheered: Poor Critchley had hard lines. What had the post done For him to bang it about so? Another inch or two would have Givei. us four, more points.., But:WayWi. ell's drop goal ,Luolly,,fh _ No one was more surprised Than the scorer. * * * * * •

Brown got a hustle on, And forced a try. Was anyone hurt in that scrum? Good old Brown wasn't. Shamrocks could not exist Among the Warrington thorns. How they pricked the visitors. * * * * * *

Marching orders were plentiful,lands'? What was the Matter at all, at all? Six men told to go—it's a bit thick. Still Flyianils' won the Manchester Shield. It is a wee crumb of comfprt for the rest. Grand try that first one, wasn't it, liorrocks? And you ran right through the ruck.' Ten men were ordered off altogether. The referee was evidently a wholesaler. Ire will retail the reason to the Union litter. "March, march, our boys are marching"—off. Was this the song the'teamS.sung?'

Weaste-were riled and wasted their energies. Who pushed F. Horrocks over the line? It was very hard lines and him on the ace of scoring. The second string of [Viands' also won. They played a great game at Gorsey-lane. Barton and Potter were in fine tern. Warrington White Rose did not like lands' style. There was to much "Potter"-ing for them. Ftylands' Ought to Win the cpp tie to-day. , But remember-7-not so much marching off. Runcorn White Star are a stiff lot. Bylands' should be quite equal to them.

Emmanuel Club.

The Emmanuel Club has now been in exist,_ ence for the past four seasons, and previous .1,0 this season has only taken part in friendly fixtures. While being able to retain most of last season's players, they have been strength- ening their weaknesses, and have signed on Harrison, - Wainwright, Hatton, O'Brien and Harding, of Orford White Star; Allen, •of Rylands' flee.; Dodd, of Latchford Recs.; J. Lawless, of St. Barnabas' ; and Green, of Latch- ford Athletic. For the coming season they have a heavy fixture list of 38 matches to /take part in. . -

..The following are the officials of the club: President, Rev. NV.. T. Jaye; treasurer, P. Broadhurst; captain, NV. Ayres; hon. secre- tary, Thomas Knight, 14, York-street, Warring- ton.

GEORGE DICKENSON GETS HIS CAP.

A county cap has been bestowed upon George Dickenson, our sturdy threequarter,.and every- body will extend their heartiest congratulations upon the veteran on this honour being obtained. Dickenson's career is so well known that it need not be recapitulated, but I must add it has been one of , the most honourable in the history of the club. It is to be hoped George will have a bumper " house " at his benefit on 4th October.,

INTERESTINg ITEMS ABOUT WARRINGTON

Honour for S. 'Ruddick.

•S. Ruddick, the smart,Warrington half-back, has been selected to \play for his native connty, Cumberland, against Lancashire, on the 29th September. The honour thus conferred upon Ruddick is well deserved, as this season he has played consistently and well.

New Player Signed On.

The Warrington Club have signed on H. Page, and he:has gone iptp training with the view of 'being given a trial. He has just left the Army, and was in the GlouTstershire Regi- ment, *here, it is understood,l* was a player in the Regimental English Union team. He is said to have the makings of a good N.U. player.

Old Officials Honoured.

A pleasant little function took place at the meeting of the Warrington Committee on Wednesday evening. This was the presenta- tion of the illuminated addresses to Messrs. H. Ashton and T. Davies, members who have done so much to build up the club. At the request Of the recipients the presentation was a private and quiet functiOn. The President, Mr. T. Locker, made the presentation, and paid a brief tribute to the work accomplished by Messrs. Ashton and Davies, who suitably acknowledged the gift..

HOWARD DAVIS NOT SUSPENDED. "Not Guilty, But Don't Do It Again!" Howard Davis has not been suspended for the alleged foul play at. Huddersfield. There is bit 'of IriShism 'about the' whole thing, and it reminds one of the Irish jury's verdict of "Not guilty, but don't do it again !"

The Committee of the Northern Union League met at the Grosvenor-Hotel-, -Manchester;, on Tuesday, and amongst the matters dealt with was that of the. Davis ease. The report of the refeilee alleged that Howard -'had interfqed with 'Gleesan; when the latter- was scoring a try in the second half of .the.makh at iludders 'field n. Monday Week. Davis appeared person- ally,And denied the allegation In. tote). 'Gleeson's statement corroborated the Warrington players' version.

The committee, hoWever, felt that they would have to whitewash the report somehow; and they came to the remarkable decision, remark- able in face of the defence made out by the War- rington man and others Who were present, that sending off the field was sufficient for the alleged offence. Warrington were ordered to pay the cost of the inquiry. t The whole business from beginning to end has been extraordinary, and the committee have not improved matters ,by their, decision., Of course, I always appreciate the necessity for the committee keeping a firm hand upon players and clubs, but the Davis case was one iniwhich the charge, one of the flimsiest, might have _been dismissed. Warrington certainly ought not to have been mulcted in the costs. These were-small, and the money spent by*War- rington has been well spent in the interests of the game. Huddersfield were fined ss. for a five minutes' late start against Warrington. This was due to a sChoolboy, match preceding the senior • • fixture

HOW RYLANDS' WON TIIE SHIELD.

'Viands' Rees played Weaste at Cadishead on Saturday to decide who should be the-holders of the Manchester Shield. Both teams were well represented. 'Viands', winning the toss, elected to play up 'the slope. Weaste kicked off before a fair attendance. In the first minute, from loose play by Weaste, A: Horrocks picked up, forced his way through the opposition, and scored. Morris failed at goal.. After this re- verse Weaste settled down to a forward game. The tackling of both 'sides was too vigorous to be coinfo,ilable. !Viands' still continued to press, F. Horrocks having hard lines in being pushed over the corner flag. :From -the pack, Broadhurst scored, Bramhall •gOaling. - .The game -after, this got very .rough, and both •sides were cautioned. From the twenty-five yards line Bramhall dropped a lovely 'goal. The game still continued to be rough, and two for- wards were given marching orders. From a mull by the Weaste full-back, Griffiths picked up and scored far• out, Bramhall failing. Half- time arrived with the score: BylanCle, 13 points; Weaste, nil. Rylands' commenced the second half down the slope. The referee from this point seemed to lose control of the players, and eight more were ordered off. Rough play was still the order, Rylands' finishing with seven men. Tries were scored by Carter, Broadhurst and Griffiths for. Rylands', and Greartrix for Weaste. The result was: Bylands', 3 goals 6 tries (24 points); Weaste, 1 goal 1 try (5 points). 'Viands' thus ; won the shield for the second time.

RYCANDS' CUP TIE.

From a shield final, Bylands' step to the first round of the Lancashire Junior Cup, and an attractive game will be played at Gorsey-lane to-day. Bylands' opponents are the Buncorn White Star, a team with a good reputation. he Star will probably put up a good fight, but 1 think Rylands' will emerge victors. The home side are to put a strong team into the field. The 'thirteen in all probability will he: A. Bramhall, back; J. Griffiths, S.-Cm;ter, k. Horrocks and F. Horrocks, threequ6rter hacks; G. IMPROVED THREEQUARTER PLAY.

The most marked improvement in the War- rington team was observed at threequarter. Ttiere were more attempts at combination than has yet been seen. This is a department which might be profitably more developed, and a part o nights qiight, with advantage, be th e devoted d training oindulging in passing. The three- quarters want to learn how to judge distance as regards their throws. Bradshaw displayed his speed, but he still was left begging for the ball too much_ Both Dickenson and Davis might -give the wing men a few more chances. Brookes was better fed, same hh ee to pit-Ch. All did some excellent work. Dicken- son was quite another man from what he was at Huddersfield. His tackling was sure, and he thealth os au effected some fine touch kicks. Davis was the weakest man in the quartette, but he shows signs of shaking down, and doubtless will Soon be• up to form. Clare was more conspicuous amongst a good sextette in theF forward line.
